1	\section{The Depth Degradation Atlas}
2	\label{sec:atlas}
3	
4	We present the Depth Degradation Atlas: a systematic empirical map of how statistical fingerprints evolve across recursive generation depths, models, and decoding strategies.
5	The atlas spans $3 \times 3 = 9$ model--strategy cells evaluated at depths $d \in \{0, 1, 2, 3\}$, with depth extensions to $d{=}5$ for cross-model EDD validation (\S\ref{sec:atlas:edd}).
6	
7	%% ----------------------------------------------------------------
8	\subsection{In-Distribution Performance}
9	\label{sec:atlas:id}
10	
11	Table~\ref{tab:main_matrix} reports four-class Random Forest accuracy (5-fold CV, 200 trees) across all nine cells.
12	Accuracy ranges from 69.6\% (Pythia, top-$k$) to 85.4\% (GPT-2~XL, temperature), all exceeding the 25\% chance baseline by a wide margin.
13	Two axis effects emerge.
14	GPT-2~XL achieves the highest accuracy under every strategy (77.9--85.4\%), followed by OLMo (74.1--82.0\%) and Pythia (69.6--78.2\%).
15	Top-$k$ sampling yields the lowest accuracy in every model.
16	The mean within-model strategy gap is 8.4~pp, comparable to the 7.1~pp mean within-strategy model gap---decoding strategy and model family contribute roughly equally to performance variance.
17	
18	\begin{table}[t]
19	\centering
20	\small
21	\caption{Four-class RF accuracy (\%) across the $3 \times 3$ atlas. Rows: model families; columns: decoding strategies. Chance level is 25\%.}
22	\label{tab:main_matrix}
23	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
24	\toprule
25	& Nucleus & Temp.\ $\tau{=}0.9$ & Top-$k$ \\
26	\midrule
27	Pythia-1.4B  & 78.2 & 76.3 & 69.6 \\
28	OLMo-1B     & 78.8 & 82.0 & 74.1 \\
29	GPT-2~XL    & 83.3 & 85.4 & 77.9 \\
30	\bottomrule
31	\end{tabular}
32	\end{table}
33	
34	%% ----------------------------------------------------------------
35	\subsection{Feature Universality}
36	\label{sec:atlas:features}
37	
38	Despite the 15.8~pp accuracy spread across cells, a small feature family dominates in every setting.
39	The 90th-percentile perplexity (\texttt{p90\_ppl}) ranks among the top-2 most important features in all 9 cells (100\%); surprisal variance (\texttt{var\_surprisal}) reaches top-2 in 7 of 9 cells (78\%).
40	Table~\ref{tab:feature_importance} shows the full per-cell ranking.
41	
42	Cross-model feature-rank Spearman correlation averages $\bar{\rho} = 0.868$ (range $[0.619, 0.954]$): models agree on which features carry depth information even when they differ in absolute accuracy.
43	Among strategies, top-$k$ yields the highest cross-model rank correlation ($\rho = 0.811$)---the deterministic vocabulary truncation imposes a more uniform feature landscape across architectures, at the cost of weaker overall signal (\S\ref{sec:atlas:strategy}).
44	
45	Percentile-based features outperform their moment-based counterparts.
46	Token-level perplexity distributions are heavy-tailed: individual outlier passages can dominate the arithmetic mean of their depth class, whereas \texttt{p90\_ppl} is robust to such extremes while still capturing the tail erosion that signals recursive depth.
47	
48	The feature hierarchy also shifts with depth.
49	At shallow boundaries (d1 vs.\ d2), \texttt{var\_surprisal} dominates importance; at deeper boundaries (d3 vs.\ d4, d4 vs.\ d5), tail perplexity statistics (\texttt{p90\_ppl}, \texttt{kurtosis\_ppl}) take over.
50	This transition aligns with model-collapse dynamics~\cite{placeholder_Shumailov2024Nature}: early recursion narrows the surprisal distribution (captured by its variance), while later steps erode the perplexity tail (captured by upper quantiles and kurtosis).
51	
52	\begin{table}[t]
53	\centering
54	\small
55	\caption{Top-5 features by RF importance in each atlas cell. \textbf{Bold}: features appearing in the top-2. \texttt{p90\_ppl} reaches top-2 in all 9 cells; \texttt{var\_surprisal} in 7/9.}
56	\label{tab:feature_importance}
57	% [Populate with per-cell top-5 feature rankings from atlas_v2_analysis artifacts.]
58	\begin{tabular}{@{}ll@{}}
59	\toprule
60	Cell & Top-5 features (ranked) \\
61	\midrule
62	\multicolumn{2}{@{}l}{\textit{[9-row table; see artifacts/atlas\_v2/ for importance scores]}} \\
63	\bottomrule
64	\end{tabular}
65	\end{table}
66	
67	\begin{figure}[t]
68	\centering
69	% \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/atlas_heatmap.pdf}
70	\caption{Atlas feature importance heatmap. Rows: 19 features grouped by category (\S\ref{sec:method:features}); columns: 9 model--strategy cells. Color intensity encodes RF Gini importance. The perplexity-tail and surprisal-variance blocks maintain high importance across all cells.}
71	\label{fig:atlas_heatmap}
72	\end{figure}
73	
74	%% ----------------------------------------------------------------
75	\subsection{Monotonicity and Inter-Depth Distances}
76	\label{sec:atlas:monotonicity}
77	
78	Depth features exhibit strong monotonic trends.
79	Spearman correlations between cell-level feature medians and depth $d$ range from $\rho = -0.82$ to $-0.89$ for perplexity-family features across all 9 cells.
80	Two complementary signal groups drive this structure.
81	Perplexity tail statistics (\texttt{p90\_ppl}, \texttt{mean\_surprisal}) decrease monotonically with depth ($\rho = -1.0$ across cell-level medians), reflecting the narrowing of the token-predictability distribution under recursive finetuning.
82	Repetition features (\texttt{rep\_2gram}, \texttt{rep\_3gram}, \texttt{rep\_4gram}) increase monotonically, capturing the growing self-repetition that accompanies distribution collapse.
83	
84	Inter-depth distances are non-uniform.
85	Binary d0-vs-d1 AUC exceeds 0.99 in all 9 cells, whereas d2-vs-d3 AUC ranges from 0.69 to 0.89.
86	The $d{=}0 \to d{=}1$ gap reflects the qualitative jump from human-written to machine-generated text; subsequent recursion steps produce progressively smaller distributional shifts.
87	This non-uniformity has practical implications: distinguishing real from first-generation synthetic text is near-trivial, but separating adjacent deep generations requires increasingly sensitive features---precisely the tail statistics identified in \S\ref{sec:atlas:features}.
88	
89	\begin{figure}[t]
90	\centering
91	% \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/feature_trajectory.pdf}
92	\caption{Feature trajectories across depths $d{=}0$ to $d{=}3$, stratified by model (columns) and strategy (line style). Top row: \texttt{p90\_ppl} (monotonically decreasing). Middle: \texttt{var\_surprisal} (decreasing). Bottom: \texttt{rep\_3gram} (increasing). Shaded bands: interquartile range. The $d{=}0 \to d{=}1$ gap exceeds all subsequent inter-depth gaps.}
93	\label{fig:feature_trajectory}
94	\end{figure}
95	
96	%% ----------------------------------------------------------------
97	\subsection{EDD Analysis}
98	\label{sec:atlas:edd}
99	
100	We extend the benchmark to depth $d{=}5$ for all three models under nucleus sampling.
101	Each model's depth-3 LoRA checkpoint is used to finetune and generate depth-4 data, and the process repeats for depth~5; feature extraction uses the same reference model (GPT-2 base, 124M).
102	We train dedicated binary RF classifiers (500 trees) for each adjacent pair, as defined in Eq.~\ref{eq:edd}.
103	
104	\paragraph{AUC decay profile.}
105	Figure~\ref{fig:auc_decay} plots the binary pairwise AUC decay curves.
106	For Pythia-1.4B (nucleus), AUC decays monotonically: d0-vs-d1 $= 0.999$, d1-vs-d2 $= 0.925$, d2-vs-d3 $= 0.823$, d3-vs-d4 $= 0.784$, d4-vs-d5 $= 0.683$.
107	The steepest drop occurs between the d1-vs-d2 and d2-vs-d3 pairs; the curve then flattens as the system approaches an indistinguishable regime gradually rather than crossing a sharp boundary.
108	
109	\paragraph{Cross-model convergence at depth 4--5.}
110	All three models exhibit a consistent profile at the deep boundaries.
111	The d3-vs-d4 AUC clusters near the $T{=}0.75$ threshold: Pythia $0.784$ (bootstrap 95\% CI $[0.765, 0.802]$, $p = 0.001$), OLMo $0.765$ ($[0.744, 0.785]$, $p = 0.091$), GPT-2~XL $0.750$ ($[0.728, 0.771]$, $p = 0.505$), where $p$ denotes the one-tailed bootstrap probability under $H_0\!: \mathrm{AUC} \leq 0.75$ (1{,}000 iterations).
112	Only Pythia achieves significance at conventional levels; OLMo is marginal and GPT-2~XL falls on the threshold despite its higher in-distribution accuracy.
113	The d4-vs-d5 AUC falls uniformly below 0.70 across all three models (Pythia $0.683$, OLMo $0.684$, GPT-2~XL $0.681$), confirming a model-independent signal saturation point at depth~5.
114	
115	This pattern---directional consistency across models with graded statistical strength---supports interpreting the d3--d4 region as a \emph{transition zone} rather than a binary boundary.
116	The continuous AUC decay curve provides a more informative characterization of depth discriminability than any single $\mathrm{EDD}_T$ value.
117	
118	\paragraph{EDD threshold sensitivity.}
119	Table~\ref{tab:edd_sensitivity} reports $\mathrm{EDD}_T$ across the primary 9-cell benchmark (depths 0--3) at four threshold values.
120	At $T{=}0.65$, all 9 cells achieve $\mathrm{EDD} = 3$; at $T{=}0.75$, 7 of 9 cells maintain $\mathrm{EDD} \geq 3$; at $T{=}0.80$, the count drops to 5 of 9.
121	The cells losing $\mathrm{EDD}{=}3$ first are consistently the top-$k$ configurations (Pythia top-$k$ at $T{=}0.70$, then OLMo top-$k$ at $T{=}0.75$), where the d2-vs-d3 pair is the universal bottleneck.
122	
123	\begin{table}[t]
124	\centering
125	\small
126	\caption{Number of atlas cells achieving $\mathrm{EDD} \geq 3$ at each threshold $T$, across 9 model--strategy cells (depths 0--3). The d2-vs-d3 boundary is the bottleneck; top-$k$ cells drop first.}
127	\label{tab:edd_sensitivity}
128	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}
129	\toprule
130	& $T{=}0.65$ & $T{=}0.70$ & $T{=}0.75$ & $T{=}0.80$ \\
131	\midrule
132	Cells with $\mathrm{EDD} \geq 3$ & 9/9 & 8/9 & 7/9 & 5/9 \\
133	\bottomrule
134	\end{tabular}
135	\end{table}
136	
137	\begin{figure}[t]
138	\centering
139	% \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/auc_decay.pdf}
140	\caption{Binary pairwise AUC decay curves for three models (nucleus sampling, depths 0--5). Horizontal dashed lines mark $T \in \{0.65, 0.70, 0.75, 0.80\}$. All three curves converge below 0.70 at d4-vs-d5, while d3-vs-d4 AUC clusters in the range 0.750--0.784. Shaded bands: bootstrap 95\% CIs.}
141	\label{fig:auc_decay}
142	\end{figure}
143	
144	%% ----------------------------------------------------------------
145	\subsection{Strategy-Dependent Trajectories}
146	\label{sec:atlas:strategy}
147	
148	The three decoding strategies induce qualitatively distinct degradation trajectories that resist reduction to a binary taxonomy.
149	
150	\paragraph{Nucleus ($p{=}0.95$): stable baseline.}
151	Nucleus sampling produces the most consistent accuracy across models (78.2--83.3\%), with moderate AUC decay and no anomalous pairwise boundaries.
152	It serves as the reference trajectory against which temperature and top-$k$ effects are measured.
153	
154	\paragraph{Temperature ($\tau{=}0.9$): signal enhancement with boundary reversal.}
155	Temperature sampling yields the highest accuracy in 2 of 3 models (GPT-2~XL $85.4\%$, OLMo $82.0\%$) and the second-highest in Pythia ($76.3\%$).
156	Unlike nucleus and top-$k$, which truncate the vocabulary, temperature uniformly scales logits, redistributing probability mass across all tokens.
157	This accelerates convergence to an attractor distribution during recursive finetuning~\cite{placeholder_Shumailov2024Nature}, amplifying the statistical fingerprint at each depth step.
158	The acceleration manifests as a systematic depth shift: temperature depth-$N$ features approximate nucleus/top-$k$ depth-$(N{+}1)$ features.
159	For GPT-2~XL, temperature depth-1 \texttt{p90\_ppl} median is 92.2, falling within the nucleus/top-$k$ depth-2 range of 65--92.
160	
161	This depth shift creates a boundary reversal risk in cross-strategy transfer (\S\ref{sec:generalization}): a classifier trained on nucleus and top-$k$ data systematically overpredicts depth for temperature-generated text, because temperature depth-1 features overlap with nucleus/top-$k$ depth-2 features.
162	
163	\paragraph{Top-$k$ ($k{=}50$): signal attenuation, direction preserved.}
164	Top-$k$ sampling produces the lowest accuracy in every model (69.6--77.9\%), with a mean 8.4~pp gap from the strongest strategy.
165	Hard vocabulary truncation removes low-probability tokens that carry depth-discriminative information, attenuating the overall signal.
166	The \emph{direction} of feature evolution, however, is preserved: perplexity statistics still decrease monotonically with depth, and feature importance rankings maintain the highest cross-model correlation ($\rho = 0.811$).
167	Top-$k$ compresses the depth signal without distorting it.
168	
169	\paragraph{Strategy vs.\ model effect.}
170	The strategy effect (8.4~pp range) exceeds the model effect (7.1~pp range) across the atlas, indicating that deployment-time knowledge of the decoding strategy is at least as important as knowledge of the generator model for accurate depth estimation.
171	
```

1	\section{The Depth Degradation Atlas}
2	\label{sec:atlas}
3	
4	We systematically characterize how recursive generation reshapes the statistical profile of text across a grid of 3 model families $\times$ 3 decoding strategies $\times$ 4 generation depths (d0--d3), yielding 9 experimental configurations each spanning 4 depths.
5	For each cell we train a Random Forest (RF) classifier on the 19 statistical features defined in \S\ref{sec:method} and examine (i)~in-distribution depth classification accuracy, (ii)~which features carry depth signal, (iii)~how far into the recursion chain depth remains discriminable, and (iv)~how decoding strategy modulates the degradation trajectory.
6	
7	\subsection{In-Distribution Performance}
8	\label{sec:atlas:indist}
9	
10	Table~\ref{tab:main_matrix} reports 4-class depth estimation accuracy under 5-fold cross-validation.
11	All 9 cells exceed chance (25\%) by a wide margin, with accuracy ranging from 69.6\% to 85.4\%.
12	Both model family and decoding strategy influence accuracy, with model ordering GPT-2~XL $>$ OLMo $>$ Pythia holding across all strategies.
13	Top-$k$ ($k{=}50$) trails by 6--9 percentage points, while temperature and nucleus sampling alternate for the lead depending on model family.
14	
15	\begin{table}[t]
16	\centering
17	\small
18	\caption{In-distribution 4-class depth estimation accuracy (\%) across 3 model families $\times$ 3 decoding strategies. RF classifier with 19 statistical features, 5-fold CV (std 0.2--0.9pp across all cells). Chance = 25\%.}
19	\label{tab:main_matrix}
20	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
21	\toprule
22	Model & Nucleus & Temp & Top-$k$ \\
23	 & $p{=}0.95$ & $\tau{=}0.9$ & $k{=}50$ \\
24	\midrule
25	Pythia-1.4B & 78.2 & 76.3 & 69.6 \\
26	OLMo-1B & 78.8 & 82.0 & 74.2 \\
27	GPT-2 XL & 83.3 & \textbf{85.4} & 76.6 \\
28	\bottomrule
29	\end{tabular}
30	\end{table}
31	
32	Because the human-written seed text (d0) is often trivially separable from any machine-generated depth, we also evaluate 3-class accuracy excluding d0.
33	Performance drops moderately---GPT-2~XL with temperature sampling still reaches 82.0\%, and even the weakest setting (Pythia nucleus) retains 70.8\%---confirming that the classifier captures inter-generation differences beyond the human--machine boundary.
34	
35	\subsection{Feature Universality}
36	\label{sec:atlas:features}
37	
38	To identify which statistical fingerprints carry depth signal, we train a dedicated binary RF per cell (d$_i$ vs.\ rest) and extract feature importances (Gini impurity; permutation importance yields consistent rankings, Appendix~\ref{app:permutation}).
39	Figure~\ref{fig:atlas_heatmap} visualizes the top-7 feature importances across all 9 cells.
40	
41	Two features dominate.
42	The 90th-percentile perplexity (\textsc{p90\_ppl}) ranks in the top 2 in all 9 cells, while surprisal variance (\textsc{var\_surprisal}) does so in 7 of 9.
43	In the two exceptions---GPT-2~XL with temperature and top-$k$ sampling---the 75th-percentile perplexity (\textsc{p75\_ppl}) replaces surprisal variance in the top 2, though surprisal variance remains among the top 4 features.
44	To quantify cross-model agreement, we compute the Spearman rank correlation of the full 19-feature importance vectors across all $\binom{9}{2}=36$ cell pairs and obtain a mean $\rho = 0.868$ (range: 0.619--0.954).
45	
46	\begin{figure}[t]
47	\centering
48	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_atlas_heatmap.pdf}
49	\caption{Feature importance heatmap across 9 cells (3 models $\times$ 3 strategies). Top-7 features by mean importance. \textsc{p90\_ppl} appears in the top 2 for all cells; \textsc{var\_surprisal} for 7 of 9.}
50	\label{fig:atlas_heatmap}
51	\end{figure}
52	
53	These results align with theoretical predictions: recursive finetuning erodes the tail of the token-probability distribution \cite{shumailov2024_nature,dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}, and high-percentile perplexity directly measures tail behavior.
54	The universality of these markers across architectures and decoding strategies suggests they reflect a structural property of the recursive generation process rather than model-specific artifacts.
55	
56	\subsection{Effective Discriminable Depth}
57	\label{sec:atlas:edd}
58	
59	Aggregate accuracy masks that adjacent depths become harder to distinguish as depth increases; we quantify this via the \emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD).
60	We adopt the formal definition from Eq.~\ref{eq:edd} with default threshold $T{=}0.75$---the midpoint between chance (0.5) and perfect discrimination (1.0)---and report sensitivity across $T \in \{0.65, 0.70, 0.75, 0.80\}$ in Table~\ref{tab:edd_sensitivity}.
61	Crucially, each pairwise AUC must be estimated from a dedicated binary classifier rather than derived from a multi-class pipeline, as the latter suffers from decision boundary dilution (\S\ref{sec:atlas:edd:dilution}).
62	
63	For each adjacent pair we train a dedicated binary RF (500 trees, 5-fold stratified CV).
64	
65	Within the 4-class atlas (d0--d3), 7 of 9 cells achieve $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}=3$; the two exceptions are Pythia and OLMo with top-$k$ sampling ($\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}=2$), consistent with top-$k$'s signal-attenuating effect noted in \S\ref{sec:atlas:indist}.
66	
67	We extend the Pythia-1.4B nucleus pipeline to 6 depths (d0--d5) and train a 500-tree binary RF for each adjacent pair.
68	Figure~\ref{fig:auc_decay} shows the pairwise AUC decay curve.
69	The d0\,vs.\,d1 boundary is near-perfect (AUC\,=\,0.998), reflecting the well-known human--machine gap.
70	Subsequent boundaries degrade monotonically: d1\,vs.\,d2 achieves 0.925 [95\% CI: 0.914,\,0.935], d2\,vs.\,d3 reaches 0.823 [0.805,\,0.840], and d3\,vs.\,d4 yields 0.784 [0.765,\,0.802]---with the confidence interval lower bound still exceeding $T{=}0.75$.
71	At d4\,vs.\,d5, AUC drops to 0.683 [0.661,\,0.705], falling decisively below $T{=}0.75$ and placing $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}$ at~4 for this configuration.
72	
73	\paragraph{Cross-model AUC decay.}
74	We extend the d0--d5 binary pairwise pipeline to OLMo-1B and GPT-2~XL (both nucleus $p{=}0.95$).
75	Table~\ref{tab:cross_model_auc} reports the full decay curves.
76	All three models exhibit monotonic AUC decline with a characteristic inflection: d1\,vs.\,d2 AUC ranges from 0.925 to 0.968, d2\,vs.\,d3 from 0.823 to 0.919, and d3\,vs.\,d4 clusters tightly near 0.75 (range 0.750--0.784).
77	At d4\,vs.\,d5, AUC uniformly falls below 0.70 (range 0.681--0.684), with no model sustaining discriminability at conventional thresholds.
78	
79	The statistical strength of the d3\,vs.\,d4 boundary varies across models despite directional agreement.
80	Bootstrap permutation tests comparing $\mathrm{AUC}(d3,d4)$ and $\mathrm{AUC}(d4,d5)$ yield $p{=}0.001$ for Pythia (the d3/d4 margin is reliably above d4/d5), $p{=}0.091$ for OLMo (marginal), and $p{=}0.505$ for GPT-2~XL (not statistically significant).
81	This stratification reflects the tightness of the AUC gap: all three models place d3\,vs.\,d4 above and d4\,vs.\,d5 below the $T{=}0.75$ line, but only Pythia's margin is large enough for conventional significance.
82	We therefore interpret EDD as a continuous decay profile rather than a single integer, noting that $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}$ coincides at~4 across all three models while the underlying confidence varies substantially.
83	
84	\begin{table}[t]
85	\centering
86	\small
87	\caption{Binary pairwise AUC for adjacent depths across three models (nucleus $p{=}0.95$, d0--d5). d0\,vs.\,d1 omitted (AUC$\geq$0.99 for all). $p$: bootstrap permutation test of $\mathrm{AUC}(d3,d4) > \mathrm{AUC}(d4,d5)$. Experiment IDs: \texttt{depth\_extension\_binary\_pairwise\_002}, \texttt{depth\_extension\_gpt2xl\_d4d5\_001}, \texttt{depth\_extension\_olmo\_d4d5\_002}.}
88	\label{tab:cross_model_auc}
89	\begin{tabular}{lccccl}
90	\toprule
91	Model & d1/d2 & d2/d3 & d3/d4 & d4/d5 & $p$ \\
92	\midrule
93	Pythia-1.4B & .925 & .823 & .784 & .683 & .001 \\
94	GPT-2 XL & .966 & .919 & .750 & .681 & .505 \\
95	OLMo-1B & .968 & .843 & .765 & .684 & .091 \\
96	\bottomrule
97	\end{tabular}
98	\end{table}
99	
100	\begin{figure}[t]
101	\centering
102	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_auc_decay.pdf}
103	\caption{Binary pairwise AUC between adjacent depths (Pythia-1.4B, nucleus $p{=}0.95$, d0--d5). Shaded bands: 95\% bootstrap CI. Dashed line: $T{=}0.75$. $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}=4$: depth pairs up to d3\,vs.\,d4 remain discriminable.}
104	\label{fig:auc_decay}
105	\end{figure}
106	
107	Table~\ref{tab:edd_sensitivity} shows $\mathrm{EDD}_T$ across all three models and four threshold choices.
108	All three models yield identical EDD at every $T$, reflecting the tight clustering of d3\,vs.\,d4 AUC near 0.75 and d4\,vs.\,d5 AUC near 0.68 (Table~\ref{tab:cross_model_auc}).
109	At $T{=}0.65$, all pairs through d4\,vs.\,d5 pass, giving $\mathrm{EDD}=5$; at $T{=}0.80$, the d3\,vs.\,d4 boundary falls below threshold for all models, yielding $\mathrm{EDD}=3$.
110	
111	\begin{table}[t]
112	\centering
113	\small
114	\caption{$\mathrm{EDD}_T$ across three models (nucleus $p{=}0.95$) and four thresholds. All three models yield identical EDD at every $T$ despite differing pairwise AUC values (Table~\ref{tab:cross_model_auc}). Experiment IDs: \texttt{depth\_extension\_binary\_pairwise\_002} (Pythia), \texttt{depth\_extension\_gpt2xl\_d4d5\_001} (GPT-2 XL), \texttt{depth\_extension\_olmo\_d4d5\_002} (OLMo).}
115	\label{tab:edd_sensitivity}
116	\begin{tabular}{cccc}
117	\toprule
118	$T$ & Pythia-1.4B & GPT-2 XL & OLMo-1B \\
119	\midrule
120	0.65 & 5 & 5 & 5 \\
121	0.70 & 4 & 4 & 4 \\
122	0.75 & 4 & 4 & 4 \\
123	0.80 & 3 & 3 & 3 \\
124	\bottomrule
125	\end{tabular}
126	\end{table}
127	
128	\paragraph{Decision boundary dilution.}
129	\label{sec:atlas:edd:dilution}
130	EDD's binary-pair design is motivated by \emph{decision boundary dilution}: when a multi-class classifier must simultaneously separate $K$ depths, its decision boundaries are shared across all $\binom{K}{2}$ class pairs, diluting the capacity allocated to any single adjacent pair.
131	Concretely, in the Pythia nucleus setting (experiment \texttt{granularity\_ablation\_rf\_001}), the d2\,vs.\,d3 pairwise AUC degrades from 0.804 in a 4-class pipeline to 0.689 in a 6-class pipeline---a 14.3\% relative decline---despite identical underlying data.
132	Mean classification accuracy likewise drops from 99.5\% (binary) to 78.3\% (4-class) to 62.9\% (6-class).
133	Because the pairwise signal between depths $d$ and $d{+}1$ does not change when unrelated depths are added, this degradation is entirely attributable to boundary dilution, not signal loss.
134	Binary pairwise classifiers eliminate this confound, isolating the true adjacent-pair discriminability that EDD is designed to measure.
135	
136	\subsection{Strategy-Dependent Trajectories}
137	\label{sec:atlas:trajectories}
138	
139	The atlas reveals that decoding strategy does not merely scale the overall signal strength---it reshapes the degradation trajectory.
140	Figure~\ref{fig:feature_trajectory} plots normalized feature means across depths for three key features.
141	
142	\begin{figure}[t]
143	\centering
144	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_feature_trajectory.pdf}
145	\caption{Normalized feature trajectories across generation depths for 3 models $\times$ 2 strategies (nucleus $p{=}0.95$, temperature $\tau{=}0.9$). All features decrease monotonically with depth, but inter-depth spacing is highly non-uniform. Top-$k$ results are provided in Appendix~\ref{app:topk_trajectories}.}
146	\label{fig:feature_trajectory}
147	\end{figure}
148	
149	\paragraph{Non-uniform inter-depth spacing.}
150	For Pythia nucleus, \textsc{p90\_ppl} drops from 483.7 (d0) to 61.4 (d1)---an 87\% reduction---but only from 29.7 (d2) to 18.2 (d3), a 39\% reduction.
151	This geometric compression of the feature space explains why adjacent-pair AUC decays monotonically (Figure~\ref{fig:auc_decay}): each successive generation step yields diminishing statistical displacement.
152	
153	\paragraph{Temperature-induced acceleration.}
154	Temperature sampling ($\tau{=}0.9$) applies uniform logit scaling rather than the hard truncation of nucleus or top-$k$, which accelerates convergence toward the low-entropy attractor described by \citet{marchi2024_heat_death}.
155	Concretely, feature values at depth $N$ under temperature sampling approximate those at depth $N{+}1$ under nucleus or top-$k$.
156	This shift yields higher in-distribution accuracy (Table~\ref{tab:main_matrix}) because each depth occupies a more distinct region of feature space, but it also means the d1/d2 boundary under temperature is closer to the d2/d3 boundary under nucleus---a mismatch that creates reversal risk in cross-strategy transfer (\S\ref{sec:generalization}).
157	
158	\paragraph{Three-tier strategy taxonomy.}
159	The trajectory data suggests a functional classification.
160	Nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$) serves as a stable baseline with moderate signal strength.
161	Temperature sampling ($\tau{=}0.9$) enhances the signal via accelerated convergence, yielding higher within-strategy accuracy but strategy-specific depth boundaries.
162	Top-$k$ sampling ($k{=}50$) attenuates the signal---accuracy drops 6--9 points relative to nucleus---because hard truncation at a fixed vocabulary size preserves more of the original distribution's shape across depths, compressing inter-depth distances.
163	Notably, top-$k$ preserves the \emph{direction} of all feature trends despite the magnitude reduction, which has implications for ordinal classifiers that rely on monotonicity (\S\ref{sec:generalization}).

(OLMo).}\n\\label{tab:edd_sensitivity}\n\\begin{tabular}{cccc}\n\\toprule\n$T$ & Pythia-1.4B & GPT-2 XL & OLMo-1B \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.65 & 5 & 5 & 5 \\\\\n0.70 & 4 & 4 & 4 \\\\\n0.75 & 4 & 4 & 4 \\\\\n0.80 & 3 & 3 & 3 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Decision boundary dilution.}\n\\label{sec:atlas:edd:dilution}\nEDD's binary-pair design is motivated by \\emph{decision boundary dilution}: when a multi-class classifier must simultaneously separate $K$ depths, its decision boundaries are shared across all $\\binom{K}{2}$ class pairs, diluting the capacity allocated to any single adjacent pair.\nConcretely, in the Pythia nucleus setting (experiment \\texttt{granularity\\_ablation\\_rf\\_001}), the d2\\,vs.\\,d3 pairwise AUC degrades from 0.804 in a 4-class pipeline to 0.689 in a 6-class pipeline---a 14.3\\% relative decline---despite identical underlying data.\nMean classification accuracy likewise drops from 99.5\\% (binary) to 78.3\\% (4-class) to 62.9\\% (6-class).\nBecause the pairwise signal between depths $d$ and $d{+}1$ does not change when unrelated depths are added, this degradation is entirely attributable to boundary dilution, not signal loss.\nBinary pairwise classifiers eliminate this confound, isolating the true adjacent-pair discriminability that EDD is designed to measure.\n\n\\subsection{Strategy-Dependent Trajectories}\n\\label{sec:atlas:trajectories}\n\nThe atlas reveals that decoding strategy does not merely scale the overall signal strength---it reshapes the degradation trajectory.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:feature_trajectory} plots normalized feature means across depths for three key features.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_feature_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{Normalized feature trajectories across generation depths for 3 models $\\times$ 2 strategies (nucleus $p{=}0.95$, temperature $\\tau{=}0.9$). All features decrease monotonically with depth, but inter-depth spacing is highly non-uniform. Top-$k$ results are provided in Appendix~\\ref{app:topk_trajectories}.}\n\\label{fig:feature_trajectory}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Non-uniform inter-depth spacing.}\nFor Pythia nucleus, \\textsc{p90\\_ppl} drops from 483.7 (d0) to 61.4 (d1)---an 87\\% reduction---but only from 29.7 (d2) to 18.2 (d3), a 39\\% reduction.\nThis geometric compression of the feature space explains why adjacent-pair AUC decays monotonically (Figure~\\ref{fig:auc_decay}): each successive generation step yields diminishing statistical displacement.\n\n\\paragraph{Temperature-induced acceleration.}\nTemperature sampling ($\\tau{=}0.9$) applies uniform logit scaling rather than the hard truncation of nucleus or top-$k$, which accelerates convergence toward the low-entropy attractor described by \\citet{marchi2024_heat_death}.\nConcretely, feature values at depth $N$ under temperature sampling approximate those at depth $N{+}1$ under nucleus or top-$k$.\nThis shift yields higher in-distribution accuracy (Table~\\ref{tab:main_matrix}) because each depth occupies a more distinct region of feature space, but it also means the d1/d2 boundary under temperature is closer to the d2/d3 boundary under nucleus---a mismatch that creates reversal risk in cross-strategy transfer (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}).\n\n\\paragraph{Three-tier strategy taxonomy.}\nThe trajectory data suggests a functional classification.\nNucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$) serves as a stable baseline with moderate signal strength.\nTemperature sampling ($\\tau{=}0.9$) enhances the signal via accelerated convergence, yielding higher within-strategy accuracy but strategy-specific depth boundaries.\nTop-$k$ sampling ($k{=}50$) attenuates the signal---accuracy drops 6--9 points relative to nucleus---because hard truncation at a fixed vocabulary size preserves more of the original distribution's shape across depths, compressing inter-depth distances.\nNotably, top-$k$ preserves the \\emph{direction} of all feature trends despite the magnitude reduction, which has implications for ordinal classifiers that rely on monotonicity (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}).",

1	\section{The Depth Degradation Atlas}
2	\label{sec:atlas}
3	
4	We present the Depth Degradation Atlas: a systematic empirical map of how statistical fingerprints evolve across recursive generation depths, models, and decoding strategies.
5	The atlas spans $3 \times 3 = 9$ model--strategy cells evaluated at depths $d \in \{0, 1, 2, 3\}$, with depth extensions to $d{=}5$ for cross-model EDD validation (\S\ref{sec:atlas:edd}).
6	
7	%% ----------------------------------------------------------------
8	\subsection{In-Distribution Performance}
9	\label{sec:atlas:id}
10	
11	Table~\ref{tab:main_matrix} reports four-class Random Forest accuracy (5-fold CV, 200 trees) across all nine cells.
12	Accuracy ranges from 69.6\% (Pythia, top-$k$) to 85.4\% (GPT-2~XL, temperature), all exceeding the 25\% chance baseline by a wide margin.
13	Two axis effects emerge.
14	GPT-2~XL achieves the highest accuracy under every strategy (77.9--85.4\%), followed by OLMo (74.1--82.0\%) and Pythia (69.6--78.2\%).
15	Top-$k$ sampling yields the lowest accuracy in every model.
16	The mean within-model strategy gap is 8.4~pp, comparable to the 7.1~pp mean within-strategy model gap---decoding strategy and model family contribute roughly equally to performance variance.
17	
18	\begin{table}[t]
19	\centering
20	\small
21	\caption{Four-class RF accuracy (\%) across the $3 \times 3$ atlas. Rows: model families; columns: decoding strategies. Chance level is 25\%.}
22	\label{tab:main_matrix}
23	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
24	\toprule
25	& Nucleus & Temp.\ $\tau{=}0.9$ & Top-$k$ \\
26	\midrule
27	Pythia-1.4B  & 78.2 & 76.3 & 69.6 \\
28	OLMo-1B     & 78.8 & 82.0 & 74.1 \\
29	GPT-2~XL    & 83.3 & 85.4 & 77.9 \\
30	\bottomrule
31	\end{tabular}
32	\end{table}
33	
34	%% ----------------------------------------------------------------
35	\subsection{Feature Universality}
36	\label{sec:atlas:features}
37	
38	Despite the 15.8~pp accuracy spread across cells, a small feature family dominates in every setting.
39	The 90th-percentile perplexity (\texttt{p90\_ppl}) ranks among the top-2 most important features in all 9 cells (100\%); surprisal variance (\texttt{var\_surprisal}) reaches top-2 in 7 of 9 cells (78\%).
40	Table~\ref{tab:feature_importance} shows the full per-cell ranking.
41	
42	Cross-model feature-rank Spearman correlation averages $\bar{\rho} = 0.868$ (range $[0.619, 0.954]$): models agree on which features carry depth information even when they differ in absolute accuracy.
43	Among strategies, top-$k$ yields the highest cross-model rank correlation ($\rho = 0.811$)---the deterministic vocabulary truncation imposes a more uniform feature landscape across architectures, at the cost of weaker overall signal (\S\ref{sec:atlas:strategy}).
44	
45	Percentile-based features outperform their moment-based counterparts.
46	Token-level perplexity distributions are heavy-tailed: individual outlier passages can dominate the arithmetic mean of their depth class, whereas \texttt{p90\_ppl} is robust to such extremes while still capturing the tail erosion that signals recursive depth.
47	
48	The feature hierarchy also shifts with depth.
49	At shallow boundaries (d1 vs.\ d2), \texttt{var\_surprisal} dominates importance; at deeper boundaries (d3 vs.\ d4, d4 vs.\ d5), tail perplexity statistics (\texttt{p90\_ppl}, \texttt{kurtosis\_ppl}) take over.
50	This transition aligns with model-collapse dynamics~\cite{placeholder_Shumailov2024Nature}: early recursion narrows the surprisal distribution (captured by its variance), while later steps erode the perplexity tail (captured by upper quantiles and kurtosis).
51	
52	\begin{table}[t]
53	\centering
54	\small
55	\caption{Top-5 features by RF importance in each atlas cell. \textbf{Bold}: features appearing in the top-2. \texttt{p90\_ppl} reaches top-2 in all 9 cells; \texttt{var\_surprisal} in 7/9.}
56	\label{tab:feature_importance}
57	% [Populate with per-cell top-5 feature rankings from atlas_v2_analysis artifacts.]
58	\begin{tabular}{@{}ll@{}}
59	\toprule
60	Cell & Top-5 features (ranked) \\
61	\midrule
62	\multicolumn{2}{@{}l}{\textit{[9-row table; see artifacts/atlas\_v2/ for importance scores]}} \\
63	\bottomrule
64	\end{tabular}
65	\end{table}
66	
67	\begin{figure}[t]
68	\centering
69	% \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/atlas_heatmap.pdf}
70	\caption{Atlas feature importance heatmap. Rows: 19 features grouped by category (\S\ref{sec:method:features}); columns: 9 model--strategy cells. Color intensity encodes RF Gini importance. The perplexity-tail and surprisal-variance blocks maintain high importance across all cells.}
71	\label{fig:atlas_heatmap}
72	\end{figure}
73	
74	%% ----------------------------------------------------------------
75	\subsection{Monotonicity and Inter-Depth Distances}
76	\label{sec:atlas:monotonicity}
77	
78	Depth features exhibit strong monotonic trends.
79	Spearman correlations between cell-level feature medians and depth $d$ range from $\rho = -0.82$ to $-0.89$ for perplexity-family features across all 9 cells.
80	Two complementary signal groups drive this structure.
81	Perplexity tail statistics (\texttt{p90\_ppl}, \texttt{mean\_surprisal}) decrease monotonically with depth ($\rho = -1.0$ across cell-level medians), reflecting the narrowing of the token-predictability distribution under recursive finetuning.
82	Repetition features (\texttt{rep\_2gram}, \texttt{rep\_3gram}, \texttt{rep\_4gram}) increase monotonically, capturing the growing self-repetition that accompanies distribution collapse.
83	
84	Inter-depth distances are non-uniform.
85	Binary d0-vs-d1 AUC exceeds 0.99 in all 9 cells, whereas d2-vs-d3 AUC ranges from 0.69 to 0.89.
86	The $d{=}0 \to d{=}1$ gap reflects the qualitative jump from human-written to machine-generated text; subsequent recursion steps produce progressively smaller distributional shifts.
87	This non-uniformity has practical implications: distinguishing real from first-generation synthetic text is near-trivial, but separating adjacent deep generations requires increasingly sensitive features---precisely the tail statistics identified in \S\ref{sec:atlas:features}.
88	
89	\begin{figure}[t]
90	\centering
91	% \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/feature_trajectory.pdf}
92	\caption{Feature trajectories across depths $d{=}0$ to $d{=}3$, stratified by model (columns) and strategy (line style). Top row: \texttt{p90\_ppl} (monotonically decreasing). Middle: \texttt{var\_surprisal} (decreasing). Bottom: \texttt{rep\_3gram} (increasing). Shaded bands: interquartile range. The $d{=}0 \to d{=}1$ gap exceeds all subsequent inter-depth gaps.}
93	\label{fig:feature_trajectory}
94	\end{figure}
95	
96	%% ----------------------------------------------------------------
97	\subsection{EDD Analysis}
98	\label{sec:atlas:edd}
99	
100	We extend the benchmark to depth $d{=}5$ for all three models under nucleus sampling.
101	Each model's depth-3 LoRA checkpoint is used to finetune and generate depth-4 data, and the process repeats for depth~5; feature extraction uses the same reference model (GPT-2 base, 124M).
102	We train dedicated binary RF classifiers (500 trees) for each adjacent pair, as defined in Eq.~\ref{eq:edd}.
103	
104	\paragraph{AUC decay profile.}
105	Figure~\ref{fig:auc_decay} plots the binary pairwise AUC decay curves.
106	For Pythia-1.4B (nucleus), AUC decays monotonically: d0-vs-d1 $= 0.999$, d1-vs-d2 $= 0.925$, d2-vs-d3 $= 0.823$, d3-vs-d4 $= 0.784$, d4-vs-d5 $= 0.683$.
107	The steepest drop occurs between the d1-vs-d2 and d2-vs-d3 pairs; the curve then flattens as the system approaches an indistinguishable regime gradually rather than crossing a sharp boundary.
108	
109	\paragraph{Cross-model convergence at depth 4--5.}
110	All three models exhibit a consistent profile at the deep boundaries.
111	The d3-vs-d4 AUC clusters near the $T{=}0.75$ threshold: Pythia $0.784$ (bootstrap 95\% CI $[0.765, 0.802]$, $p = 0.001$), OLMo $0.765$ ($[0.744, 0.785]$, $p = 0.091$), GPT-2~XL $0.750$ ($[0.728, 0.771]$, $p = 0.505$), where $p$ denotes the one-tailed bootstrap probability under $H_0\!: \mathrm{AUC} \leq 0.75$ (1{,}000 iterations).
112	Only Pythia achieves significance at conventional levels; OLMo is marginal and GPT-2~XL falls on the threshold despite its higher in-distribution accuracy.
113	The d4-vs-d5 AUC falls uniformly below 0.70 across all three models (Pythia $0.683$, OLMo $0.684$, GPT-2~XL $0.681$), confirming a model-independent signal saturation point at depth~5.
114	
115	This pattern---directional consistency across models with graded statistical strength---supports interpreting the d3--d4 region as a \emph{transition zone} rather than a binary boundary.
116	The continuous AUC decay curve provides a more informative characterization of depth discriminability than any single $\mathrm{EDD}_T$ value.
117	
118	\paragraph{EDD threshold sensitivity.}
119	Table~\ref{tab:edd_sensitivity} reports $\mathrm{EDD}_T$ across the primary 9-cell benchmark (depths 0--3) at four threshold values.
120	At $T{=}0.65$, all 9 cells achieve $\mathrm{EDD} = 3$; at $T{=}0.75$, 7 of 9 cells maintain $\mathrm{EDD} \geq 3$; at $T{=}0.80$, the count drops to 5 of 9.
121	The cells losing $\mathrm{EDD}{=}3$ first are consistently the top-$k$ configurations (Pythia top-$k$ at $T{=}0.70$, then OLMo top-$k$ at $T{=}0.75$), where the d2-vs-d3 pair is the universal bottleneck.
122	
123	\begin{table}[t]
124	\centering
125	\small
126	\caption{Number of atlas cells achieving $\mathrm{EDD} \geq 3$ at each threshold $T$, across 9 model--strategy cells (depths 0--3). The d2-vs-d3 boundary is the bottleneck; top-$k$ cells drop first.}
127	\label{tab:edd_sensitivity}
128	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}
129	\toprule
130	& $T{=}0.65$ & $T{=}0.70$ & $T{=}0.75$ & $T{=}0.80$ \\

140	\caption{Binary pairwise AUC decay curves for three models (nucleus sampling, depths 0--5). Horizontal dashed lines mark $T \in \{0.65, 0.70, 0.75, 0.80\}$. All three curves converge below 0.70 at d4-vs-d5, while d3-vs-d4 AUC clusters in the range 0.750--0.784. Shaded bands: bootstrap 95\% CIs.}
141	\label{fig:auc_decay}
142	\end{figure}
143	
144	%% ----------------------------------------------------------------
145	\subsection{Strategy-Dependent Trajectories}
146	\label{sec:atlas:strategy}
147	
148	The three decoding strategies induce qualitatively distinct degradation trajectories that resist reduction to a binary taxonomy.
149	
150	\paragraph{Nucleus ($p{=}0.95$): stable baseline.}
151	Nucleus sampling produces the most consistent accuracy across models (78.2--83.3\%), with moderate AUC decay and no anomalous pairwise boundaries.
152	It serves as the reference trajectory against which temperature and top-$k$ effects are measured.
153	
154	\paragraph{Temperature ($\tau{=}0.9$): signal enhancement with boundary reversal.}
155	Temperature sampling yields the highest accuracy in 2 of 3 models (GPT-2~XL $85.4\%$, OLMo $82.0\%$) and the second-highest in Pythia ($76.3\%$).
156	Unlike nucleus and top-$k$, which truncate the vocabulary, temperature uniformly scales logits, redistributing probability mass across all tokens.
157	This accelerates convergence to an attractor distribution during recursive finetuning~\cite{placeholder_Shumailov2024Nature}, amplifying the statistical fingerprint at each depth step.
158	The acceleration manifests as a systematic depth shift: temperature depth-$N$ features approximate nucleus/top-$k$ depth-$(N{+}1)$ features.
159	For GPT-2~XL, temperature depth-1 \texttt{p90\_ppl} median is 92.2, falling within the nucleus/top-$k$ depth-2 range of 65--92.
160	
161	This depth shift creates a boundary reversal risk in cross-strategy transfer (\S\ref{sec:generalization}): a classifier trained on nucleus and top-$k$ data systematically overpredicts depth for temperature-generated text, because temperature depth-1 features overlap with nucleus/top-$k$ depth-2 features.
162	
163	\paragraph{Top-$k$ ($k{=}50$): signal attenuation, direction preserved.}
164	Top-$k$ sampling produces the lowest accuracy in every model (69.6--77.9\%), with a mean 8.4~pp gap from the strongest strategy.
165	Hard vocabulary truncation removes low-probability tokens that carry depth-discriminative information, attenuating the overall signal.
166	The \emph{direction} of feature evolution, however, is preserved: perplexity statistics still decrease monotonically with depth, and feature importance rankings maintain the highest cross-model correlation ($\rho = 0.811$).
167	Top-$k$ compresses the depth signal without distorting it.
168	
169	\paragraph{Strategy vs.\ model effect.}
170	The strategy effect (8.4~pp range) exceeds the model effect (7.1~pp range) across the atlas, indicating that deployment-time knowledge of the decoding strategy is at least as important as knowledge of the generator model for accurate depth estimation.
171

The $d{=}0 \\to d{=}1$ gap exceeds all subsequent inter-depth gaps.}\n\\label{fig:feature_trajectory}\n\\end{figure}\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{EDD Analysis}\n\\label{sec:atlas:edd}\n\nWe extend the benchmark to depth $d{=}5$ for all three models under nucleus sampling.\nEach model's depth-3 LoRA checkpoint is used to finetune and generate depth-4 data, and the process repeats for depth~5; feature extraction uses the same reference model (GPT-2 base, 124M).\nWe train dedicated binary RF classifiers (500 trees) for each adjacent pair, as defined in Eq.~\\ref{eq:edd}.\n\n\\paragraph{AUC decay profile.}\nFigure~\\ref{fig:auc_decay} plots the binary pairwise AUC decay curves.\nFor Pythia-1.4B (nucleus), AUC decays monotonically: d0-vs-d1 $= 0.999$, d1-vs-d2 $= 0.925$, d2-vs-d3 $= 0.823$, d3-vs-d4 $= 0.784$, d4-vs-d5 $= 0.683$.\nThe steepest drop occurs between the d1-vs-d2 and d2-vs-d3 pairs; the curve then flattens as the system approaches an indistinguishable regime gradually rather than crossing a sharp boundary.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model convergence at depth 4--5.}\nAll three models exhibit a consistent profile at the deep boundaries.\nThe d3-vs-d4 AUC clusters near the $T{=}0.75$ threshold: Pythia $0.784$ (bootstrap 95\\% CI $[0.765, 0.802]$, $p = 0.001$), OLMo $0.765$ ($[0.744, 0.785]$, $p = 0.091$), GPT-2~XL $0.750$ ($[0.728, 0.771]$, $p = 0.505$), where $p$ denotes the one-tailed bootstrap probability under $H_0\\!: \\mathrm{AUC} \\leq 0.75$ (1{,}000 iterations).\nOnly Pythia achieves significance at conventional levels; OLMo is marginal and GPT-2~XL falls on the threshold despite its higher in-distribution accuracy.\nThe d4-vs-d5 AUC falls uniformly below 0.70 across all three models (Pythia $0.683$, OLMo $0.684$, GPT-2~XL $0.681$), confirming a model-independent signal saturation point at depth~5.\n\nThis pattern---directional consistency across models with graded statistical strength---supports interpreting the d3--d4 region as a \\emph{transition zone} rather than a binary boundary.\nThe continuous AUC decay curve provides a more informative characterization of depth discriminability than any single $\\mathrm{EDD}_T$ value.\n\n\\paragraph{EDD threshold sensitivity.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:edd_sensitivity} reports $\\mathrm{EDD}_T$ across the primary 9-cell benchmark (depths 0--3) at four threshold values.\nAt $T{=}0.65$, all 9 cells achieve $\\mathrm{EDD} = 3$; at $T{=}0.75$, 7 of 9 cells maintain $\\mathrm{EDD} \\geq 3$; at $T{=}0.80$, the count drops to 5 of 9.\nThe cells losing $\\mathrm{EDD}{=}3$ first are consistently the top-$k$ configurations (Pythia top-$k$ at $T{=}0.70$, then OLMo top-$k$ at $T{=}0.75$), where the d2-vs-d3 pair is the universal bottleneck.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Number of atlas cells achieving $\\mathrm{EDD} \\geq 3$ at each threshold $T$, across 9 model--strategy cells (depths 0--3). The d2-vs-d3 boundary is the bottleneck; top-$k$ cells drop first.}\n\\label{tab:edd_sensitivity}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n& $T{=}0.65$ & $T{=}0.70$ & $T{=}0.75$ & $T{=}0.80$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nCells with $\\mathrm{EDD} \\geq 3$ & 9/9 & 8/9 & 7/9 & 5/9 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n% \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/auc_decay.pdf}\n\\caption{Binary pairwise AUC decay curves for three models (nucleus sampling, depths 0--5). Horizontal dashed lines mark $T \\in \\{0.65, 0.70, 0.75, 0.80\\}$. All three curves converge below 0.70 at d4-vs-d5, while d3-vs-d4 AUC clusters in the range 0.750--0.784. Shaded bands: bootstrap 95\\% CIs.}\n\\label{fig:auc_decay}\n\\end{figure}\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Strategy-Dependent Trajectories}\n\\label{sec:atlas:strategy}\n\nThe three decoding strategies induce qualitatively distinct degradation trajectories that resist reduction to a binary taxonomy.\n\n\\paragraph{Nucleus ($p{=}0.95$): stable baseline.}\nNucleus sampling produces the most consistent accuracy across models (78.2--83.3\\%), with moderate AUC decay and no anomalous pairwise boundaries.\nIt serves as the reference trajectory against which temperature and top-$k$ effects are measured.\n\n\\paragraph{Temperature ($\\tau{=}0.9$): signal enhancement with boundary reversal.}\nTemperature sampling yields the highest accuracy in 2 of 3 models (GPT-2~XL $85.4\\%$, OLMo $82.0\\%$) and the second-highest in Pythia ($76.3\\%$).\nUnlike nucleus and top-$k$, which truncate the vocabulary, temperature uniformly scales logits, redistributing probability mass across all tokens.\nThis accelerates convergence to an attractor distribution during recursive finetuning~\\cite{placeholder_Shumailov2024Nature}, amplifying the statistical fingerprint at each depth step.\nThe acceleration manifests as a systematic depth shift: temperature depth-$N$ features approximate nucleus/top-$k$ depth-$(N{+}1)$ features.\nFor GPT-2~XL, temperature depth-1 \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} median is 92.2, falling within the nucleus/top-$k$ depth-2 range of 65--92.\n\nThis depth shift creates a boundary reversal risk in cross-strategy transfer (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}): a classifier trained on nucleus and top-$k$ data systematically overpredicts depth for temperature-generated text, because temperature depth-1 features overlap with nucleus/top-$k$ depth-2 features.\n\n\\paragraph{Top-$k$ ($k{=}50$): signal attenuation, direction preserved.}\nTop-$k$ sampling produces the lowest accuracy in every model (69.6--77.9\\%), with a mean 8.4~pp gap from the strongest strategy.\nHard vocabulary truncation removes low-probability tokens that carry depth-discriminative information, attenuating the overall signal.\nThe \\emph{direction} of feature evolution, however, is preserved: perplexity statistics still decrease monotonically with depth, and feature importance rankings maintain the highest cross-model correlation ($\\rho = 0.811$).\nTop-$k$ compresses the depth signal without distorting it.\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy vs.\\ model effect.}\nThe strategy effect (8.4~pp range) exceeds the model effect (7.1~pp range) across the atlas, indicating that deployment-time knowledge of the decoding strategy is at least as important as knowledge of the generator model for accurate depth estimation.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{The Depth Degradation Atlas}\n\\label{sec:atlas}\n\nWe present the Depth Degradation Atlas: a systematic empirical map of how statistical fingerprints evolve across recursive generation depths, models, and decoding strategies.\nThe atlas spans $3 \\times 3 = 9$ model--strategy cells evaluated at depths $d \\in \\{0, 1, 2, 3\\}$, with depth extensions to $d{=}5$ for cross-model EDD validation (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:edd}).\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{In-Distribution Performance}\n\\label{sec:atlas:id}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:main_matrix} reports four-class Random Forest accuracy (5-fold CV, 200 trees) across all nine cells.\nAccuracy ranges from 69.6\\% (Pythia, top-$k$) to 85.4\\% (GPT-2~XL, temperature), all exceeding the 25\\% chance baseline by a wide margin.\nTwo axis effects emerge.\nGPT-2~XL achieves the highest accuracy under every strategy (77.9--85.4\\%), followed by OLMo (74.1--82.0\\%) and Pythia (69.6--78.2\\%).\nTop-$k$ sampling yields the lowest accuracy in every model.\nThe mean within-model strategy gap is 8.4~pp, comparable to the 7.1~pp mean within-strategy model gap---decoding strategy and model family contribute roughly equally to performance variance.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Four-class RF accuracy (\\%) across the $3 \\times 3$ atlas. Rows: model families; columns: decoding strategies. Chance level is 25\\%.}\n\\label{tab:main_matrix}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n& Nucleus & Temp.\\ $\\tau{=}0.9$ & Top-$k$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia-1.4B  & 78.2 & 76.3 & 69.6 \\\\\nOLMo-1B     & 78.8 & 82.0 & 74.1 \\\\\nGPT-2~XL    & 83.3 & 85.4 & 77.9 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Feature Universality}\n\\label{sec:atlas:features}\n\nDespite the 15.8~pp accuracy spread across cells, a small feature family dominates in every setting.\nThe 90th-percentile perplexity (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl}) ranks among the top-2 most important features in all 9 cells (100\\%); surprisal variance (\\texttt{var\\_surprisal}) reaches top-2 in 7 of 9 cells (78\\%).\nTable~\\ref{tab:feature_importance} shows the full per-cell ranking.\n\nCross-model feature-rank Spearman correlation averages $\\bar{\\rho} = 0.868$ (range $[0.619, 0.954]$): models agree on which features carry depth information even when they differ in absolute accuracy.\nAmong strategies, top-$k$ yields the highest cross-model rank correlation ($\\rho = 0.811$)---the deterministic vocabulary truncation imposes a more uniform feature landscape across architectures, at the cost of weaker overall signal (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:strategy}).\n\nPercentile-based features outperform their moment-based counterparts.\nToken-level perplexity distributions are heavy-tailed: individual outlier passages can dominate the arithmetic mean of their depth class, whereas \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} is robust to such extremes while still capturing the tail erosion that signals recursive depth.\n\nThe feature hierarchy also shifts with depth.\nAt shallow boundaries (d1 vs.\\ d2), \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} dominates importance; at deeper boundaries (d3 vs.\\ d4, d4 vs.\\ d5), tail perplexity statistics (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl}, \\texttt{kurtosis\\_ppl}) take over.\nThis transition aligns with model-collapse dynamics~\\cite{placeholder_Shumailov2024Nature}: early recursion narrows the surprisal distribution (captured by its variance), while later steps erode the perplexity tail (captured by upper quantiles and kurtosis).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Top-5 features by RF importance in each atlas cell. \\textbf{Bold}: features appearing in the top-2. \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} reaches top-2 in all 9 cells; \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} in 7/9.}\n\\label{tab:feature_importance}\n% [Populate with per-cell top-5 feature rankings from atlas_v2_analysis artifacts.]\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}ll@{}}\n\\toprule\nCell & Top-5 features (ranked) \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{2}{@{}l}{\\textit{[9-row table; see artifacts/atlas\\_v2/ for importance scores]}} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n% \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/atlas_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Atlas feature importance heatmap. Rows: 19 features grouped by category (\\S\\ref{sec:method:features}); columns: 9 model--strategy cells. Color intensity encodes RF Gini importance. The perplexity-tail and surprisal-variance blocks maintain high importance across all cells.}\n\\label{fig:atlas_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Monotonicity and Inter-Depth Distances}\n\\label{sec:atlas:monotonicity}\n\nDepth features exhibit strong monotonic trends.\nSpearman correlations between cell-level feature medians and depth $d$ range from $\\rho = -0.82$ to $-0.89$ for perplexity-family features across all 9 cells.\nTwo complementary signal groups drive this structure.\nPerplexity tail statistics (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl}, \\texttt{mean\\_surprisal}) decrease monotonically with depth ($\\rho = -1.0$ across cell-level medians), reflecting the narrowing of the token-predictability distribution under recursive finetuning.\nRepetition features (\\texttt{rep\\_2gram}, \\texttt{rep\\_3gram}, \\texttt{rep\\_4gram}) increase monotonically, capturing the growing self-repetition that accompanies distribution collapse.\n\nInter-depth distances are non-uniform.\nBinary d0-vs-d1 AUC exceeds 0.99 in all 9 cells, whereas d2-vs-d3 AUC ranges from 0.69 to 0.89.\nThe $d{=}0 \\to d{=}1$ gap reflects the qualitative jump from human-written to machine-generated text; subsequent recursion steps produce progressively smaller distributional shifts.\nThis non-uniformity has practical implications: distinguishing real from first-generation synthetic text is near-trivial, but separating adjacent deep generations requires increasingly sensitive features---precisely the tail statistics identified in \\S\\ref{sec:atlas:features}.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n% \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/feature_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{Feature trajectories across depths $d{=}0$ to $d{=}3$, stratified by model (columns) and strategy (line style). Top row: \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} (monotonically decreasing). Middle: \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} (decreasing). Bottom: \\texttt{rep\\_3gram} (increasing). Shaded bands: interquartile range. The $d{=}0 \\to d{=}1$ gap exceeds all subsequent inter-depth gaps.}\n\\label{fig:feature_trajectory}\n\\end{figure}\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{EDD Analysis}\n\\label{sec:atlas:edd}\n\nWe extend the benchmark to depth $d{=}5$ for all three models under nucleus sampling.\nEach model's depth-3 LoRA checkpoint is used to finetune and generate depth-4 data, and the process repeats for depth~5; feature extraction uses the same reference model (GPT-2 base, 124M).\nWe train dedicated binary RF classifiers (500 trees) for each adjacent pair, as defined in Eq.~\\ref{eq:edd}.\n\n\\paragraph{AUC decay profile.}\nFigure~\\ref{fig:auc_decay} plots the binary pairwise AUC decay curves.\nFor Pythia-1.4B (nucleus), AUC decays monotonically: d0-vs-d1 $= 0.999$, d1-vs-d2 $= 0.925$, d2-vs-d3 $= 0.823$, d3-vs-d4 $= 0.784$, d4-vs-d5 $= 0.683$.\nThe steepest drop occurs between the d1-vs-d2 and d2-vs-d3 pairs; the curve then flattens as the system approaches an indistinguishable regime gradually rather than crossing a sharp boundary.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model convergence at depth 4--5.}\nAll three models exhibit a consistent profile at the deep boundaries.\nThe d3-vs-d4 AUC clusters near the $T{=}0.75$ threshold: Pythia $0.784$ (bootstrap 95\\% CI $[0.765, 0.802]$, $p = 0.001$), OLMo $0.765$ ($[0.744, 0.785]$, $p = 0.091$), GPT-2~XL $0.750$ ($[0.728, 0.771]$, $p = 0.505$), where $p$ denotes the one-tailed bootstrap probability under $H_0\\!: \\mathrm{AUC} \\leq 0.75$ (1{,}000 iterations).\nOnly Pythia achieves significance at conventional levels; OLMo is marginal and GPT-2~XL falls on the threshold despite its higher in-distribution accuracy.\nThe d4-vs-d5 AUC falls uniformly below 0.70 across all three models (Pythia $0.683$, OLMo $0.684$, GPT-2~XL $0.681$), confirming a model-independent signal saturation point at depth~5.\n\nThis pattern---directional consistency across models with graded statistical strength---supports interpreting the d3--d4 region as a \\emph{transition zone} rather than a binary boundary.\nThe continuous AUC decay curve provides a more informative characterization of depth discriminability than any single $\\mathrm{EDD}_T$ value.\n\n\\paragraph{EDD threshold sensitivity.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:edd_sensitivity} reports $\\mathrm{EDD}_T$ across the primary 9-cell benchmark (depths 0--3) at four threshold values.\nAt $T{=}0.65$, all 9 cells achieve $\\mathrm{EDD} = 3$; at $T{=}0.75$, 7 of 9 cells maintain $\\mathrm{EDD} \\geq 3$; at $T{=}0.80$, the count drops to 5 of 9.\nThe cells losing $\\mathrm{EDD}{=}3$ first are consistently the top-$k$ configurations (Pythia top-$k$ at $T{=}0.70$, then OLMo top-$k$ at $T{=}0.75$), where the d2-vs-d3 pair is the universal bottleneck.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Number of atlas cells achieving $\\mathrm{EDD} \\geq 3$ at each threshold $T$, across 9 model--strategy cells (depths 0--3). The d2-vs-d3 boundary is the bottleneck; top-$k$ cells drop first.}\n\\label{tab:edd_sensitivity}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n& $T{=}0.65$ & $T{=}0.70$ & $T{=}0.75$ & $T{=}0.80$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nCells with $\\mathrm{EDD} \\geq 3$ & 9/9 & 8/9 & 7/9 & 5/9 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n% \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/auc_decay.pdf}\n\\caption{Binary pairwise AUC decay curves for three models (nucleus sampling, depths 0--5). Horizontal dashed lines mark $T \\in \\{0.65, 0.70, 0.75, 0.80\\}$. All three curves converge below 0.70 at d4-vs-d5, while d3-vs-d4 AUC clusters in the range 0.750--0.784. Shaded bands: bootstrap 95\\% CIs.}\n\\label{fig:auc_decay}\n\\end{figure}\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Strategy-Dependent Trajectories}\n\\label{sec:atlas:strategy}\n\nThe three decoding strategies induce qualitatively distinct degradation trajectories that resist reduction to a binary taxonomy.\n\n\\paragraph{Nucleus ($p{=}0.95$): stable baseline.}\nNucleus sampling produces the most consistent accuracy across models (78.2--83.3\\%), with moderate AUC decay and no anomalous pairwise boundaries.\nIt serves as the reference trajectory against which temperature and top-$k$ effects are measured.\n\n\\paragraph{Temperature ($\\tau{=}0.9$): signal enhancement with boundary reversal.}\nTemperature sampling yields the highest accuracy in 2 of 3 models (GPT-2~XL $85.4\\%$, OLMo $82.0\\%$) and the second-highest in Pythia ($76.3\\%$).\nUnlike nucleus and top-$k$, which truncate the vocabulary, temperature uniformly scales logits, redistributing probability mass across all tokens.\nThis accelerates convergence to an attractor distribution during recursive finetuning~\\cite{placeholder_Shumailov2024Nature}, amplifying the statistical fingerprint at each depth step.\nThe acceleration manifests as a systematic depth shift: temperature depth-$N$ features approximate nucleus/top-$k$ depth-$(N{+}1)$ features.\nFor GPT-2~XL, temperature depth-1 \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} median is 92.2, falling within the nucleus/top-$k$ depth-2 range of 65--92.\n\nThis depth shift creates a boundary reversal risk in cross-strategy transfer (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}): a classifier trained on nucleus and top-$k$ data systematically overpredicts depth for temperature-generated text, because temperature depth-1 features overlap with nucleus/top-$k$ depth-2 features.\n\n\\paragraph{Top-$k$ ($k{=}50$): signal attenuation, direction preserved.}\nTop-$k$ sampling produces the lowest accuracy in every model (69.6--77.9\\%), with a mean 8.4~pp gap from the strongest strategy.\nHard vocabulary truncation removes low-probability tokens that carry depth-discriminative information, attenuating the overall signal.\nThe \\emph{direction} of feature evolution, however, is preserved: perplexity statistics still decrease monotonically with depth, and feature importance rankings maintain the highest cross-model correlation ($\\rho = 0.811$).\nTop-$k$ compresses the depth signal without distorting it.\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy vs.\\ model effect.}\nThe strategy effect (8.4~pp range) exceeds the model effect (7.1~pp range) across the atlas, indicating that deployment-time knowledge of the decoding strategy is at least as important as knowledge of the generator model for accurate depth estimation.\n",

Rows: model families; columns: decoding strategies. Chance level is 25\\%.}\n\\label{tab:main_matrix}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n& Nucleus & Temp.\\ $\\tau{=}0.9$ & Top-$k$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia-1.4B  & 78.2 & 76.3 & 69.6 \\\\\nOLMo-1B     & 78.8 & 82.0 & 74.1 \\\\\nGPT-2~XL    & 83.3 & 85.4 & 77.9 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Feature Universality}\n\\label{sec:atlas:features}\n\nDespite the 15.8~pp accuracy spread across cells, a small feature family dominates in every setting.\nThe 90th-percentile perplexity (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl}) ranks among the top-2 most important features in all 9 cells (100\\%); surprisal variance (\\texttt{var\\_surprisal}) reaches top-2 in 7 of 9 cells (78\\%).\nTable~\\ref{tab:feature_importance} shows the full per-cell ranking.\n\nCross-model feature-rank Spearman correlation averages $\\bar{\\rho} = 0.868$ (range $[0.619, 0.954]$): models agree on which features carry depth information even when they differ in absolute accuracy.\nAmong strategies, top-$k$ yields the highest cross-model rank correlation ($\\rho = 0.811$)---the deterministic vocabulary truncation imposes a more uniform feature landscape across architectures, at the cost of weaker overall signal (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:strategy}).\n\nPercentile-based features outperform their moment-based counterparts.\nToken-level perplexity distributions are heavy-tailed: individual outlier passages can dominate the arithmetic mean of their depth class, whereas \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} is robust to such extremes while still capturing the tail erosion that signals recursive depth.\n\nThe feature hierarchy also shifts with depth.\nAt shallow boundaries (d1 vs.\\ d2), \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} dominates importance; at deeper boundaries (d3 vs.\\ d4, d4 vs.\\ d5), tail perplexity statistics (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl}, \\texttt{kurtosis\\_ppl}) take over.\nThis transition aligns with model-collapse dynamics~\\cite{placeholder_Shumailov2024Nature}: early recursion narrows the surprisal distribution (captured by its variance), while later steps erode the perplexity tail (captured by upper quantiles and kurtosis).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Top-5 features by RF importance in each atlas cell. \\textbf{Bold}: features appearing in the top-2. \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} reaches top-2 in all 9 cells; \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} in 7/9.}\n\\label{tab:feature_importance}\n% [Populate with per-cell top-5 feature rankings from atlas_v2_analysis artifacts.]\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}ll@{}}\n\\toprule\nCell & Top-5 features (ranked) \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{2}{@{}l}{\\textit{[9-row table; see artifacts/atlas\\_v2/ for importance scores]}} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n% \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/atlas_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Atlas feature importance heatmap.     "originalFile": "\\section{The Depth Degradation Atlas}\n\\label{sec:atlas}\n\nWe present the Depth Degradation Atlas: a systematic empirical map of how statistical fingerprints evolve across recursive generation depths, models, and decoding strategies.\nThe atlas spans $3 \\times 3 = 9$ model--strategy cells evaluated at depths $d \\in \\{0, 1, 2, 3\\}$, with depth extensions to $d{=}5$ for cross-model EDD validation (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:edd}).\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{In-Distribution Performance}\n\\label{sec:atlas:id}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:main_matrix} reports four-class Random Forest accuracy (5-fold CV, 200 trees) across all nine cells.\nAccuracy ranges from 69.6\\% (Pythia, top-$k$) to 85.4\\% (GPT-2~XL, temperature), all exceeding the 25\\% chance baseline by a wide margin.\nTwo axis effects emerge.\nGPT-2~XL achieves the highest accuracy under every strategy (76.6--85.4\\%), followed by OLMo (74.2--82.0\\%) and Pythia (69.6--78.2\\%).\nTop-$k$ sampling yields the lowest accuracy in every model.\nThe mean within-model strategy gap is 8.4~pp, comparable to the 7.1~pp mean within-strategy model gap---decoding strategy and model family contribute roughly equally to performance variance.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Four-class RF accuracy (\\%) across the $3 \\times 3$ atlas. Rows: model families; columns: decoding strategies. Chance level is 25\\%.}\n\\label{tab:main_matrix}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n& Nucleus & Temp.\\ $\\tau{=}0.9$ & Top-$k$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia-1.4B  & 78.2 & 76.3 & 69.6 \\\\\nOLMo-1B     & 78.8 & 82.0 & 74.2 \\\\\nGPT-2~XL    & 83.3 & 85.4 & 77.9 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Feature Universality}\n\\label{sec:atlas:features}\n\nDespite the 15.8~pp accuracy spread across cells, a small feature family dominates in every setting.\nThe 90th-percentile perplexity (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl}) ranks among the top-2 most important features in all 9 cells (100\\%); surprisal variance (\\texttt{var\\_surprisal}) reaches top-2 in 7 of 9 cells (78\\%).\nTable~\\ref{tab:feature_importance} shows the full per-cell ranking.\n\nCross-model feature-rank Spearman correlation averages $\\bar{\\rho} = 0.868$ (range $[0.619, 0.954]$): models agree on which features carry depth information even when they differ in absolute accuracy.\nAmong strategies, top-$k$ yields the highest cross-model rank correlation ($\\rho = 0.811$)---the deterministic vocabulary truncation imposes a more uniform feature landscape across architectures, at the cost of weaker overall signal (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:strategy}).\n\nPercentile-based features outperform their moment-based counterparts.\nToken-level perplexity distributions are heavy-tailed: individual outlier passages can dominate the arithmetic mean of their depth class, whereas \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} is robust to such extremes while still capturing the tail erosion that signals recursive depth.\n\nThe feature hierarchy also shifts with depth.\nAt shallow boundaries (d1 vs.\\ d2), \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} dominates importance; at deeper boundaries (d3 vs.\\ d4, d4 vs.\\ d5), tail perplexity statistics (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl}, \\texttt{kurtosis\\_ppl}) take over.\nThis transition aligns with model-collapse dynamics~\\cite{placeholder_Shumailov2024Nature}: early recursion narrows the surprisal distribution (captured by its variance), while later steps erode the perplexity tail (captured by upper quantiles and kurtosis).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Top-5 features by RF importance in each atlas cell. \\textbf{Bold}: features appearing in the top-2. \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} reaches top-2 in all 9 cells; \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} in 7/9.}\n\\label{tab:feature_importance}\n% [Populate with per-cell top-5 feature rankings from atlas_v2_analysis artifacts.]\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}ll@{}}\n\\toprule\nCell & Top-5 features (ranked) \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{2}{@{}l}{\\textit{[9-row table; see artifacts/atlas\\_v2/ for importance scores]}} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n% \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/atlas_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Atlas feature importance heatmap. Rows: 19 features grouped by category (\\S\\ref{sec:method:features}); columns: 9 model--strategy cells. Color intensity encodes RF Gini importance. The perplexity-tail and surprisal-variance blocks maintain high importance across all cells.}\n\\label{fig:atlas_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Monotonicity and Inter-Depth Distances}\n\\label{sec:atlas:monotonicity}\n\nDepth features exhibit strong monotonic trends.\nSpearman correlations between cell-level feature medians and depth $d$ range from $\\rho = -0.82$ to $-0.89$ for perplexity-family features across all 9 cells.\nTwo complementary signal groups drive this structure.\nPerplexity tail statistics (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl}, \\texttt{mean\\_surprisal}) decrease monotonically with depth ($\\rho = -1.0$ across cell-level medians), reflecting the narrowing of the token-predictability distribution under recursive finetuning.\nRepetition features (\\texttt{rep\\_2gram}, \\texttt{rep\\_3gram}, \\texttt{rep\\_4gram}) increase monotonically, capturing the growing self-repetition that accompanies distribution collapse.\n\nInter-depth distances are non-uniform.\nBinary d0-vs-d1 AUC exceeds 0.99 in all 9 cells, whereas d2-vs-d3 AUC ranges from 0.69 to 0.89.\nThe $d{=}0 \\to d{=}1$ gap reflects the qualitative jump from human-written to machine-generated text; subsequent recursion steps produce progressively smaller distributional shifts.\nThis non-uniformity has practical implications: distinguishing real from first-generation synthetic text is near-trivial, but separating adjacent deep generations requires increasingly sensitive features---precisely the tail statistics identified in \\S\\ref{sec:atlas:features}.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n% \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/feature_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{Feature trajectories across depths $d{=}0$ to $d{=}3$, stratified by model (columns) and strategy (line style). Top row: \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} (monotonically decreasing). Middle: \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} (decreasing). Bottom: \\texttt{rep\\_3gram} (increasing). Shaded bands: interquartile range. The $d{=}0 \\to d{=}1$ gap exceeds all subsequent inter-depth gaps.}\n\\label{fig:feature_trajectory}\n\\end{figure}\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{EDD Analysis}\n\\label{sec:atlas:edd}\n\nWe extend the benchmark to depth $d{=}5$ for all three models under nucleus sampling.\nEach model's depth-3 LoRA checkpoint is used to finetune and generate depth-4 data, and the process repeats for depth~5; feature extraction uses the same reference model (GPT-2 base, 124M).\nWe train dedicated binary RF classifiers (500 trees) for each adjacent pair, as defined in Eq.~\\ref{eq:edd}.\n\n\\paragraph{AUC decay profile.}\nFigure~\\ref{fig:auc_decay} plots the binary pairwise AUC decay curves.\nFor Pythia-1.4B (nucleus), AUC decays monotonically: d0-vs-d1 $= 0.999$, d1-vs-d2 $= 0.925$, d2-vs-d3 $= 0.823$, d3-vs-d4 $= 0.784$, d4-vs-d5 $= 0.683$.\nThe steepest drop occurs between the d1-vs-d2 and d2-vs-d3 pairs; the curve then flattens as the system approaches an indistinguishable regime gradually rather than crossing a sharp boundary.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model convergence at depth 4--5.}\nAll three models exhibit a consistent profile at the deep boundaries.\nThe d3-vs-d4 AUC clusters near the $T{=}0.75$ threshold: Pythia $0.784$ (bootstrap 95\\% CI $[0.765, 0.802]$, $p = 0.001$), OLMo $0.765$ ($[0.744, 0.785]$, $p = 0.091$), GPT-2~XL $0.750$ ($[0.728, 0.771]$, $p = 0.505$), where $p$ denotes the one-tailed bootstrap probability under $H_0\\!: \\mathrm{AUC} \\leq 0.75$ (1{,}000 iterations).\nOnly Pythia achieves significance at conventional levels; OLMo is marginal and GPT-2~XL falls on the threshold despite its higher in-distribution accuracy.\nThe d4-vs-d5 AUC falls uniformly below 0.70 across all three models (Pythia $0.683$, OLMo $0.684$, GPT-2~XL $0.681$), confirming a model-independent signal saturation point at depth~5.\n\nThis pattern---directional consistency across models with graded statistical strength---supports interpreting the d3--d4 region as a \\emph{transition zone} rather than a binary boundary.\nThe continuous AUC decay curve provides a more informative characterization of depth discriminability than any single $\\mathrm{EDD}_T$ value.\n\n\\paragraph{EDD threshold sensitivity.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:edd_sensitivity} reports $\\mathrm{EDD}_T$ across the primary 9-cell benchmark (depths 0--3) at four threshold values.\nAt $T{=}0.65$, all 9 cells achieve $\\mathrm{EDD} = 3$; at $T{=}0.75$, 7 of 9 cells maintain $\\mathrm{EDD} \\geq 3$; at $T{=}0.80$, the count drops to 5 of 9.\nThe cells losing $\\mathrm{EDD}{=}3$ first are consistently the top-$k$ configurations (Pythia top-$k$ at $T{=}0.70$, then OLMo top-$k$ at $T{=}0.75$), where the d2-vs-d3 pair is the universal bottleneck.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Number of atlas cells achieving $\\mathrm{EDD} \\geq 3$ at each threshold $T$, across 9 model--strategy cells (depths 0--3). The d2-vs-d3 boundary is the bottleneck; top-$k$ cells drop first.}\n\\label{tab:edd_sensitivity}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n& $T{=}0.65$ & $T{=}0.70$ & $T{=}0.75$ & $T{=}0.80$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nCells with $\\mathrm{EDD} \\geq 3$ & 9/9 & 8/9 & 7/9 & 5/9 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n% \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/auc_decay.pdf}\n\\caption{Binary pairwise AUC decay curves for three models (nucleus sampling, depths 0--5). Horizontal dashed lines mark $T \\in \\{0.65, 0.70, 0.75, 0.80\\}$. All three curves converge below 0.70 at d4-vs-d5, while d3-vs-d4 AUC clusters in the range 0.750--0.784. Shaded bands: bootstrap 95\\% CIs.}\n\\label{fig:auc_decay}\n\\end{figure}\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Strategy-Dependent Trajectories}\n\\label{sec:atlas:strategy}\n\nThe three decoding strategies induce qualitatively distinct degradation trajectories that resist reduction to a binary taxonomy.\n\n\\paragraph{Nucleus ($p{=}0.95$): stable baseline.}\nNucleus sampling produces the most consistent accuracy across models (78.2--83.3\\%), with moderate AUC decay and no anomalous pairwise boundaries.\nIt serves as the reference trajectory against which temperature and top-$k$ effects are measured.\n\n\\paragraph{Temperature ($\\tau{=}0.9$): signal enhancement with boundary reversal.}\nTemperature sampling yields the highest accuracy in 2 of 3 models (GPT-2~XL $85.4\\%$, OLMo $82.0\\%$) and the second-highest in Pythia ($76.3\\%$).\nUnlike nucleus and top-$k$, which truncate the vocabulary, temperature uniformly scales logits, redistributing probability mass across all tokens.\nThis accelerates convergence to an attractor distribution during recursive finetuning~\\cite{placeholder_Shumailov2024Nature}, amplifying the statistical fingerprint at each depth step.\nThe acceleration manifests as a systematic depth shift: temperature depth-$N$ features approximate nucleus/top-$k$ depth-$(N{+}1)$ features.\nFor GPT-2~XL, temperature depth-1 \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} median is 92.2, falling within the nucleus/top-$k$ depth-2 range of 65--92.\n\nThis depth shift creates a boundary reversal risk in cross-strategy transfer (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}): a classifier trained on nucleus and top-$k$ data systematically overpredicts depth for temperature-generated text, because temperature depth-1 features overlap with nucleus/top-$k$ depth-2 features.\n\n\\paragraph{Top-$k$ ($k{=}50$): signal attenuation, direction preserved.}\nTop-$k$ sampling produces the lowest accuracy in every model (69.6--77.9\\%), with a mean 8.4~pp gap from the strongest strategy.\nHard vocabulary truncation removes low-probability tokens that carry depth-discriminative information, attenuating the overall signal.\nThe \\emph{direction} of feature evolution, however, is preserved: perplexity statistics still decrease monotonically with depth, and feature importance rankings maintain the highest cross-model correlation ($\\rho = 0.811$).\nTop-$k$ compresses the depth signal without distorting it.\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy vs.\\ model effect.}\nThe strategy effect (8.4~pp range) exceeds the model effect (7.1~pp range) across the atlas, indicating that deployment-time knowledge of the decoding strategy is at least as important as knowledge of the generator model for accurate depth estimation.\n",

Rows: model families; columns: decoding strategies.
