surprisal variance (\\texttt{var\\_surprisal}) reaches top-2 in 7 of 9 cells (78\\%).\nTable~\\ref{tab:feature_importance} shows the full per-cell ranking.\n\nCross-model feature-rank Spearman correlation averages $\\bar{\\rho} = 0.868$ (range $[0.619, 0.954]$): models agree on which features carry depth information even when they differ in absolute accuracy.\nAmong strategies, top-$k$ yields the highest cross-model rank correlation ($\\rho = 0.811$)---the deterministic vocabulary truncation imposes a more uniform feature landscape across architectures, at the cost of weaker overall signal (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:strategy}).\n\nPercentile-based features outperform their moment-based counterparts.\nToken-level perplexity distributions are heavy-tailed: individual outlier passages can dominate the arithmetic mean of their depth class, whereas \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} is robust to such extremes while still capturing the tail erosion that signals recursive depth.\n\nThe feature hierarchy also shifts with depth.\nAt shallow boundaries (d1 vs.\\ d2), \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} dominates importance; at deeper boundaries (d3 vs.\\ d4, d4 vs.\\ d5), tail perplexity statistics (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl}, \\texttt{kurtosis\\_ppl}) take over.\nThis transition aligns with model-collapse dynamics~\\cite{shumailov2024_nature}: early recursion narrows the surprisal distribution (captured by its variance), while later steps erode the perplexity tail (captured by upper quantiles and kurtosis).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Top-5 features by RF importance in each atlas cell. \\textbf{Bold}: features appearing in the top-2. \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} reaches top-2 in all 9 cells; \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} in 7/9.}\n\\label{tab:feature_importance}\n% [Populate with per-cell top-5 feature rankings from atlas_v2_analysis artifacts.]\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}ll@{}}\n\\toprule\nCell & Top-5 features (ranked) \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{2}{@{}l}{\\textit{[9-row table; see artifacts/atlas\\_v2/ for importance scores]}} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n% \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/atlas_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Atlas feature importance heatmap. Rows: 19 features grouped by category (\\S\\ref{sec:method:features}); columns: 9 model--strategy cells. Color intensity encodes RF Gini importance. The perplexity-tail and surprisal-variance blocks maintain high importance across all cells.}\n\\label{fig:atlas_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Monotonicity and Inter-Depth Distances}\n\\label{sec:atlas:monotonicity}\n\nDepth features exhibit strong monotonic trends.\nSpearman correlations between cell-level feature medians and depth $d$ range from $\\rho = -0.82$ to $-0.89$ for perplexity-family features across all 9 cells.\nTwo complementary signal groups drive this structure.\nPerplexity tail statistics (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl}, \\texttt{mean\\_surprisal}) decrease monotonically with depth ($\\rho = -1.0$ across cell-level medians), reflecting the narrowing of the token-predictability distribution under recursive finetuning.\nRepetition features (\\texttt{rep\\_2gram}, \\texttt{rep\\_3gram}, \\texttt{rep\\_4gram}) increase monotonically, capturing the growing self-repetition that accompanies distribution collapse.\n\nInter-depth distances are non-uniform.\nBinary d0-vs-d1 AUC exceeds 0.99 in all 9 cells, whereas d2-vs-d3 AUC ranges from 0.69 to 0.89.\nThe $d{=}0 \\to d{=}1$ gap reflects the qualitative jump from human-written to machine-generated text; subsequent recursion steps produce progressively smaller distributional shifts.\nThis non-uniformity has practical implications: distinguishing real from first-generation synthetic text is near-trivial, but separating adjacent deep generations requires increasingly sensitive features---precisely the tail statistics identified in \\S\\ref{sec:atlas:features}.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n% \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/feature_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{Feature trajectories across depths $d{=}0$ to $d{=}3$, stratified by model (columns) and strategy (line style). feature extraction uses the same reference model (GPT-2 base, 124M).\nWe train dedicated binary RF classifiers (500 trees) for each adjacent pair, as defined in Eq.~\\ref{eq:edd}.\n\n\\paragraph{AUC decay profile.}\nFigure~\\ref{fig:auc_decay} plots the binary pairwise AUC decay curves.\nFor Pythia-1.4B (nucleus), AUC decays monotonically: d0-vs-d1 $= 0.999$, d1-vs-d2 $= 0.925$, d2-vs-d3 $= 0.823$, d3-vs-d4 $= 0.784$, d4-vs-d5 $= 0.683$.\nThe steepest drop occurs between the d1-vs-d2 and d2-vs-d3 pairs; the curve then flattens as the system approaches an indistinguishable regime gradually rather than crossing a sharp boundary.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model convergence at depth 4--5.}\nAll three models exhibit a consistent profile at the deep boundaries.\nThe d3-vs-d4 AUC clusters near the $T{=}0.75$ threshold: Pythia $0.784$ (bootstrap 95\\% CI $[0.765, 0.802]$, $p = 0.001$), OLMo $0.765$ ($[0.744, 0.785]$, $p = 0.091$), GPT-2~XL $0.750$ ($[0.728, 0.771]$, $p = 0.505$), where $p$ denotes the one-tailed bootstrap probability under $H_0\\!: \\mathrm{AUC} \\leq 0.75$ (1{,}000 iterations).\nOnly Pythia achieves significance at conventional levels; OLMo is marginal and GPT-2~XL falls on the threshold despite its higher in-distribution accuracy.\nThe d4-vs-d5 AUC falls uniformly below 0.70 across all three models (Pythia $0.683$, OLMo $0.684$, GPT-2~XL $0.681$), confirming a model-independent signal saturation point at depth~5.\n\nThis pattern---directional consistency across models with graded statistical strength---supports interpreting the d3--d4 region as a \\emph{transition zone} rather than a binary boundary.\nThe continuous AUC decay curve provides a more informative characterization of depth discriminability than any single $\\mathrm{EDD}_T$ value.\n\n\\paragraph{EDD threshold sensitivity.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:edd_sensitivity} reports $\\mathrm{EDD}_T$ across the primary 9-cell benchmark (depths 0--3) at four threshold values.\nAt $T{=}0.65$, all 9 cells achieve $\\mathrm{EDD} = 3$; at $T{=}0.75$, 7 of 9 cells maintain $\\mathrm{EDD} \\geq 3$; at $T{=}0.80$, the count drops to 5 of 9.\nThe cells losing $\\mathrm{EDD}{=}3$ first are consistently the top-$k$ configurations (Pythia top-$k$ at $T{=}0.70$, then OLMo top-$k$ at $T{=}0.75$), where the d2-vs-d3 pair is the universal bottleneck.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Number of atlas cells achieving $\\mathrm{EDD} \\geq 3$ at each threshold $T$, across 9 model--strategy cells (depths 0--3). Horizontal dashed lines mark $T \\in \\{0.65, 0.70, 0.75, 0.80\\}$. All three curves converge below 0.70 at d4-vs-d5, while d3-vs-d4 AUC clusters in the range 0.750--0.784. {

  "old_string": "\\end{figure}\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Strategy-Dependent Trajectories}\n\\label{sec:atlas:strategy}",
  "new_string": "\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Decision boundary dilution.}\n\\label{sec:atlas:edd:dilution}\nEDD's binary-pair design is motivated by \\emph{decision boundary dilution}: when a multi-class classifier must simultaneously separate $K$ depths, its decision boundaries are shared across all $\\binom{K}{2}$ class pairs, diluting the capacity allocated to any single adjacent pair.\nConcretely, in the Pythia nucleus setting (experiment \\texttt{granularity\\_ablation\\_rf\\_001}), the d2\\,vs.\\,d3 pairwise AUC degrades from 0.804 in a 4-class pipeline to 0.689 in a 6-class pipeline---a 14.3\\% relative decline---despite identical underlying data.\nMean classification accuracy likewise drops from 99.5\\% (binary) to 78.3\\% (4-class) to 62.9\\% (6-class).\nBecause the pairwise signal between depths $d$ and $d{+}1$ does not change when unrelated depths are added, this degradation is entirely attributable to boundary dilution, not signal loss.\nBinary pairwise classifiers eliminate this confound, isolating the true adjacent-pair discriminability that EDD is designed to measure.\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Strategy-Dependent Trajectories}\n\\label{sec:atlas:strategy}"
}
```

    "newString": "\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Decision boundary dilution.}\n\\label{sec:atlas:edd:dilution}\nEDD's binary-pair design is motivated by \\emph{decision boundary dilution}: when a multi-class classifier must simultaneously separate $K$ depths, its decision boundaries are shared across all $\\binom{K}{2}$ class pairs, diluting the capacity allocated to any single adjacent pair.\nConcretely, in the Pythia nucleus setting (experiment \\texttt{granularity\\_ablation\\_rf\\_001}), the d2\\,vs.\\,d3 pairwise AUC degrades from 0.804 in a 4-class pipeline to 0.689 in a 6-class pipeline---a 14.3\\% relative decline---despite identical underlying data.\nMean classification accuracy likewise drops from 99.5\\% (binary) to 78.3\\% (4-class) to 62.9\\% (6-class).\nBecause the pairwise signal between depths $d$ and $d{+}1$ does not change when unrelated depths are added, this degradation is entirely attributable to boundary dilution, not signal loss.\nBinary pairwise classifiers eliminate this confound, isolating the true adjacent-pair discriminability that EDD is designed to measure.\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Strategy-Dependent Trajectories}\n\\label{sec:atlas:strategy}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{The Depth Degradation Atlas}\n\\label{sec:atlas}\n\nWe present the Depth Degradation Atlas: a systematic empirical map of how statistical fingerprints evolve across recursive generation depths, models, and decoding strategies.\nThe atlas spans $3 \\times 3 = 9$ model--strategy cells evaluated at depths $d \\in \\{0, 1, 2, 3\\}$, with depth extensions to $d{=}5$ for cross-model EDD validation (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:edd}).\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{In-Distribution Performance}\n\\label{sec:atlas:id}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:main_matrix} reports four-class Random Forest accuracy (5-fold CV, 200 trees) across all nine cells.\nAccuracy ranges from 69.6\\% (Pythia, top-$k$) to 85.4\\% (GPT-2~XL, temperature), all exceeding the 25\\% chance baseline by a wide margin.\nTwo axis effects emerge.\nGPT-2~XL achieves the highest accuracy under every strategy (76.6--85.4\\%), followed by OLMo (74.2--82.0\\%) and Pythia (69.6--78.2\\%).\nTop-$k$ sampling yields the lowest accuracy in every model.\nThe mean within-model strategy gap is 8.4~pp, comparable to the 7.1~pp mean within-strategy model gap---decoding strategy and model family contribute roughly equally to performance variance.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Four-class RF accuracy (\\%) across the $3 \\times 3$ atlas. Rows: model families; columns: decoding strategies. Chance level is 25\\%.}\n\\label{tab:main_matrix}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n& Nucleus & Temp.\\ $\\tau{=}0.9$ & Top-$k$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia-1.4B  & 78.2 & 76.3 & 69.6 \\\\\nOLMo-1B     & 78.8 & 82.0 & 74.2 \\\\\nGPT-2~XL    & 83.3 & 85.4 & 76.6 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Feature Universality}\n\\label{sec:atlas:features}\n\nDespite the 15.8~pp accuracy spread across cells, a small feature family dominates in every setting.\nThe 90th-percentile perplexity (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl}) ranks among the top-2 most important features in all 9 cells (100\\%); surprisal variance (\\texttt{var\\_surprisal}) reaches top-2 in 7 of 9 cells (78\\%).\nTable~\\ref{tab:feature_importance} shows the full per-cell ranking.\n\nCross-model feature-rank Spearman correlation averages $\\bar{\\rho} = 0.868$ (range $[0.619, 0.954]$): models agree on which features carry depth information even when they differ in absolute accuracy.\nAmong strategies, top-$k$ yields the highest cross-model rank correlation ($\\rho = 0.811$)---the deterministic vocabulary truncation imposes a more uniform feature landscape across architectures, at the cost of weaker overall signal (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:strategy}).\n\nPercentile-based features outperform their moment-based counterparts.\nToken-level perplexity distributions are heavy-tailed: individual outlier passages can dominate the arithmetic mean of their depth class, whereas \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} is robust to such extremes while still capturing the tail erosion that signals recursive depth.\n\nThe feature hierarchy also shifts with depth.\nAt shallow boundaries (d1 vs.\\ d2), \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} dominates importance; at deeper boundaries (d3 vs.\\ d4, d4 vs.\\ d5), tail perplexity statistics (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl}, \\texttt{kurtosis\\_ppl}) take over.\nThis transition aligns with model-collapse dynamics~\\cite{shumailov2024_nature}: early recursion narrows the surprisal distribution (captured by its variance), while later steps erode the perplexity tail (captured by upper quantiles and kurtosis).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Top-5 features by RF importance in each atlas cell. \\textbf{Bold}: features appearing in the top-2. \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} reaches top-2 in all 9 cells; \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} in 7/9.}\n\\label{tab:feature_importance}\n% [Populate with per-cell top-5 feature rankings from atlas_v2_analysis artifacts.]\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}ll@{}}\n\\toprule\nCell & Top-5 features (ranked) \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{2}{@{}l}{\\textit{[9-row table; see artifacts/atlas\\_v2/ for importance scores]}} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n% \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/atlas_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Atlas feature importance heatmap. Rows: 19 features grouped by category (\\S\\ref{sec:method:features}); columns: 9 model--strategy cells. Color intensity encodes RF Gini importance. The perplexity-tail and surprisal-variance blocks maintain high importance across all cells.}\n\\label{fig:atlas_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Monotonicity and Inter-Depth Distances}\n\\label{sec:atlas:monotonicity}\n\nDepth features exhibit strong monotonic trends.\nSpearman correlations between cell-level feature medians and depth $d$ range from $\\rho = -0.82$ to $-0.89$ for perplexity-family features across all 9 cells.\nTwo complementary signal groups drive this structure.\nPerplexity tail statistics (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl}, \\texttt{mean\\_surprisal}) decrease monotonically with depth ($\\rho = -1.0$ across cell-level medians), reflecting the narrowing of the token-predictability distribution under recursive finetuning.\nRepetition features (\\texttt{rep\\_2gram}, \\texttt{rep\\_3gram}, \\texttt{rep\\_4gram}) increase monotonically, capturing the growing self-repetition that accompanies distribution collapse.\n\nInter-depth distances are non-uniform.\nBinary d0-vs-d1 AUC exceeds 0.99 in all 9 cells, whereas d2-vs-d3 AUC ranges from 0.69 to 0.89.\nThe $d{=}0 \\to d{=}1$ gap reflects the qualitative jump from human-written to machine-generated text; subsequent recursion steps produce progressively smaller distributional shifts.\nThis non-uniformity has practical implications: distinguishing real from first-generation synthetic text is near-trivial, but separating adjacent deep generations requires increasingly sensitive features---precisely the tail statistics identified in \\S\\ref{sec:atlas:features}.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n% \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/feature_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{Feature trajectories across depths $d{=}0$ to $d{=}3$, stratified by model (columns) and strategy (line style). Top row: \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} (monotonically decreasing). Middle: \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} (decreasing). Bottom: \\texttt{rep\\_3gram} (increasing). Shaded bands: interquartile range. The $d{=}0 \\to d{=}1$ gap exceeds all subsequent inter-depth gaps.}\n\\label{fig:feature_trajectory}\n\\end{figure}\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{EDD Analysis}\n\\label{sec:atlas:edd}\n\nWe extend the benchmark to depth $d{=}5$ for all three models under nucleus sampling.\nEach model's depth-3 LoRA checkpoint is used to finetune and generate depth-4 data, and the process repeats for depth~5; feature extraction uses the same reference model (GPT-2 base, 124M).\nWe train dedicated binary RF classifiers (500 trees) for each adjacent pair, as defined in Eq.~\\ref{eq:edd}.\n\n\\paragraph{AUC decay profile.}\nFigure~\\ref{fig:auc_decay} plots the binary pairwise AUC decay curves.\nFor Pythia-1.4B (nucleus), AUC decays monotonically: d0-vs-d1 $= 0.999$, d1-vs-d2 $= 0.925$, d2-vs-d3 $= 0.823$, d3-vs-d4 $= 0.784$, d4-vs-d5 $= 0.683$.\nThe steepest drop occurs between the d1-vs-d2 and d2-vs-d3 pairs; the curve then flattens as the system approaches an indistinguishable regime gradually rather than crossing a sharp boundary.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model convergence at depth 4--5.}\nAll three models exhibit a consistent profile at the deep boundaries.\nThe d3-vs-d4 AUC clusters near the $T{=}0.75$ threshold: Pythia $0.784$ (bootstrap 95\\% CI $[0.765, 0.802]$, $p = 0.001$), OLMo $0.765$ ($[0.744, 0.785]$, $p = 0.091$), GPT-2~XL $0.750$ ($[0.728, 0.771]$, $p = 0.505$), where $p$ denotes the one-tailed bootstrap probability under $H_0\\!: \\mathrm{AUC} \\leq 0.75$ (1{,}000 iterations).\nOnly Pythia achieves significance at conventional levels; OLMo is marginal and GPT-2~XL falls on the threshold despite its higher in-distribution accuracy.\nThe d4-vs-d5 AUC falls uniformly below 0.70 across all three models (Pythia $0.683$, OLMo $0.684$, GPT-2~XL $0.681$), confirming a model-independent signal saturation point at depth~5.\n\nThis pattern---directional consistency across models with graded statistical strength---supports interpreting the d3--d4 region as a \\emph{transition zone} rather than a binary boundary.\nThe continuous AUC decay curve provides a more informative characterization of depth discriminability than any single $\\mathrm{EDD}_T$ value.\n\n\\paragraph{EDD threshold sensitivity.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:edd_sensitivity} reports $\\mathrm{EDD}_T$ across the primary 9-cell benchmark (depths 0--3) at four threshold values.\nAt $T{=}0.65$, all 9 cells achieve $\\mathrm{EDD} = 3$; at $T{=}0.75$, 7 of 9 cells maintain $\\mathrm{EDD} \\geq 3$; at $T{=}0.80$, the count drops to 5 of 9.\nThe cells losing $\\mathrm{EDD}{=}3$ first are consistently the top-$k$ configurations (Pythia top-$k$ at $T{=}0.70$, then OLMo top-$k$ at $T{=}0.75$), where the d2-vs-d3 pair is the universal bottleneck.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Number of atlas cells achieving $\\mathrm{EDD} \\geq 3$ at each threshold $T$, across 9 model--strategy cells (depths 0--3). The d2-vs-d3 boundary is the bottleneck; top-$k$ cells drop first.}\n\\label{tab:edd_sensitivity}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n& $T{=}0.65$ & $T{=}0.70$ & $T{=}0.75$ & $T{=}0.80$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nCells with $\\mathrm{EDD} \\geq 3$ & 9/9 & 8/9 & 7/9 & 5/9 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n% \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/auc_decay.pdf}\n\\caption{Binary pairwise AUC decay curves for three models (nucleus sampling, depths 0--5). Horizontal dashed lines mark $T \\in \\{0.65, 0.70, 0.75, 0.80\\}$. All three curves converge below 0.70 at d4-vs-d5, while d3-vs-d4 AUC clusters in the range 0.750--0.784. Shaded bands: bootstrap 95\\% CIs.}\n\\label{fig:auc_decay}\n\\end{figure}\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Strategy-Dependent Trajectories}\n\\label{sec:atlas:strategy}\n\nThe three decoding strategies induce qualitatively distinct degradation trajectories that resist reduction to a binary taxonomy.\n\n\\paragraph{Nucleus ($p{=}0.95$): stable baseline.}\nNucleus sampling produces the most consistent accuracy across models (78.2--83.3\\%), with moderate AUC decay and no anomalous pairwise boundaries.\nIt serves as the reference trajectory against which temperature and top-$k$ effects are measured.\n\n\\paragraph{Temperature ($\\tau{=}0.9$): signal enhancement with boundary reversal.}\nTemperature sampling yields the highest accuracy in 2 of 3 models (GPT-2~XL $85.4\\%$, OLMo $82.0\\%$) and the second-highest in Pythia ($76.3\\%$).\nUnlike nucleus and top-$k$, which truncate the vocabulary, temperature uniformly scales logits, redistributing probability mass across all tokens.\nThis accelerates convergence to an attractor distribution during recursive finetuning~\\cite{shumailov2024_nature}, amplifying the statistical fingerprint at each depth step.\nThe acceleration manifests as a systematic depth shift: temperature depth-$N$ features approximate nucleus/top-$k$ depth-$(N{+}1)$ features.\nFor GPT-2~XL, temperature depth-1 \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} median is 92.2, falling within the nucleus/top-$k$ depth-2 range of 65--92.\n\nThis depth shift creates a boundary reversal risk in cross-strategy transfer (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}): a classifier trained on nucleus and top-$k$ data systematically overpredicts depth for temperature-generated text, because temperature depth-1 features overlap with nucleus/top-$k$ depth-2 features.\n\n\\paragraph{Top-$k$ ($k{=}50$): signal attenuation, direction preserved.}\nTop-$k$ sampling produces the lowest accuracy in every model (69.6--76.6\\%), with a mean 8.4~pp gap from the strongest strategy.\nHard vocabulary truncation removes low-probability tokens that carry depth-discriminative information, attenuating the overall signal.\nThe \\emph{direction} of feature evolution, however, is preserved: perplexity statistics still decrease monotonically with depth, and feature importance rankings maintain the highest cross-model correlation ($\\rho = 0.811$).\nTop-$k$ compresses the depth signal without distorting it.\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy vs.\\ model effect.}\nThe strategy effect (8.4~pp range) exceeds the model effect (7.1~pp range) across the atlas, indicating that deployment-time knowledge of the decoding strategy is at least as important as knowledge of the generator model for accurate depth estimation.\n",

    "oldString": "sec:atlas:trajectories",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Generalization and Robustness}\n\\label{sec:generalization}\n\nWe test whether the atlas results (69.6--85.4\\%) reflect genuine statistical regularities or training artifacts, evaluating four axes: classifier choice (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:baselines}), cross-model transfer (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossmodel}), cross-strategy transfer (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}), and end-to-end learning (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:e2e}).\n\n\\subsection{Baseline Comparison}\n\\label{sec:gen:baselines}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:baselines} compares classification methods on the Pythia-1.4B nucleus setting.\nThree feature-based methods---Random Forest (RF), CORAL ordinal regression \\cite{coral2020}, and DivEye \\cite{diveye2026}---all operate on the same 19 statistical features and achieve statistically indistinguishable accuracy (78.1--78.2\\%; pairwise bootstrap $p>0.05$ after Bonferroni correction, 95\\% CI within $\\pm$1.1pp).\nThese three significantly outperform CE-MLP ($p<0.001$, $+$3.8pp), which in turn trails logistic regression by $\\sim$0.1pp.\nSingle-feature regression on mean perplexity performs at chance (25.1\\%), confirming that depth estimation requires the multi-dimensional feature representation defined in \\S\\ref{sec:method}.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Comparison of classification methods on Pythia-1.4B nucleus. RF, CORAL, and DivEye use the same 19 statistical features and are statistically indistinguishable (bootstrap $p>0.05$, Bonferroni-corrected). Chance = 25\\%. 5-fold CV std: 0.2--0.9pp across all methods.}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\begin{tabular}{lc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & 4-class Acc (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nRF (19 features) & 78.2 \\\\\nCORAL & 78.1 \\\\\nDivEye & 78.2 \\\\\nCE-MLP & 74.9 \\\\\nLR & 75.0 \\\\\nDeepSeek V3.2 (zero-shot)\\footnotemark & 40.0 \\\\\nppl-regression (single feature) & 25.1 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\footnotetext{Evaluated on 200 samples (approximately 50 per class) from the Pythia-1.4B nucleus cell only, due to API cost; 95\\% CI: $\\pm$6.8pp. All other methods use the full 20{,}000-sample set.}\n\\end{table}\n\nThat three structurally different classifiers achieve identical performance confirms that the discriminative signal resides in the 19 features, not the classifier architecture; a zero-shot LLM baseline (DeepSeek V3.2) reaches only 40.0\\%.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:confusion_matrix} shows d0 is near-perfectly identified in both settings; errors concentrate at the d2/d3 boundary in-distribution and spread across d1--d3 under cross-model transfer.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_confusion_matrix.pdf}\n\\caption{Confusion matrices for the best in-distribution setting (GPT-2 XL, temp $\\tau{=}0.9$, 85.4\\%) and the best cross-model leave-one-out transfer (OLMo holdout, 69.2\\%). d0 recall exceeds 99\\% in both; errors spread across the d1--d3 range under transfer.}\n\\label{fig:confusion_matrix}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Transfer}\n\\label{sec:gen:crossmodel}\n\nWe perform leave-one-model-family-out evaluation: train on two families (nucleus only), test on the held-out third (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-model transfer (leave-one-out, nucleus only). Train on 2 model families, test on held-out 3rd. Chance = 25\\%.}\n\\label{tab:cross_model}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nHoldout & Acc & d0 & d1 & d2 & d3 \\\\\n & (\\%) & Rec. & Rec. & Rec. & Rec. \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia & 62.4 & 100.0 & 49.3 & 26.8 & 73.6 \\\\\nOLMo & \\textbf{69.2} & 99.3 & 75.5 & 43.3 & 58.8 \\\\\nGPT-2 XL & 67.6 & 98.5 & 74.7 & 52.6 & 44.5 \\\\\n\\midrule\nMean & 66.4 & 99.3 & 66.5 & 40.9 & 59.0 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nMean transfer accuracy reaches 66.4\\% (2.7$\\times$ chance), though d0 recall ($>$98\\%) inflates the headline; d2 recall drops to 26.8\\% (Pythia holdout), making it the most confusable class.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Strategy Transfer}\n\\label{sec:gen:crossstrategy}\n\nWe train on two decoding strategies and test on the held-out third (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_strategy}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-strategy transfer (leave-one-out). Train on 2 strategies, test on held-out 3rd. Chance = 25\\%.}\n\\label{tab:cross_strategy}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nHoldout & Pythia & OLMo & GPT-2 XL \\\\\n & Acc (\\%) & Acc (\\%) & Acc (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nnucleus & \\textbf{69.4} & \\textbf{65.4} & \\textbf{59.9} \\\\\ntemp ($\\tau{=}0.9$) & 62.8 & 58.2 & 56.1 \\\\\ntop-$k$ ($k{=}50$) & 51.5 & 54.9 & 53.6 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nMean cross-strategy accuracy (59.1\\%) falls 7 points below cross-model transfer (66.4\\%), indicating that strategy-induced distribution shifts pose a greater generalization challenge than architectural differences.\nAs with cross-model transfer, d0 recall remains high ($\\geq$89.5\\%) across all holdout settings, inflating the headline accuracy; d2 recall ranges from 3.7\\% to 19\\%, confirming mid-chain depths as the primary confusion locus across both transfer axes.\n\n\\paragraph{Temperature-induced boundary inversion.}\nTraining on nucleus + top-$k$ and testing on temperature yields d1v2 AUC of 0.129 for GPT-2~XL and 0.253 for OLMo---both below 0.5, indicating that the d1/d2 decision boundary \\emph{inverts} (Pythia retains a degraded 0.576), consistent with the accelerated convergence in \\S\\ref{sec:atlas:trajectories}.\nZ-score normalization \\cite{sugiyama2007_importance_weighting} improves logistic regression cross-strategy accuracy (25\\%$\\to$61\\%) but does not recover the inversion---all three models worsen (GPT-2~XL 0.50$\\to$0.24, OLMo 0.50$\\to$0.19, Pythia 0.63$\\to$0.52)---confirming a genuine geometry-level shift rather than a correctable scale difference.\n\n\\subsection{End-to-End Learning}\n\\label{sec:gen:e2e}\n\nWe finetune DeBERTa-v3-base \\cite{he2021_deberta_v3} and RoBERTa-base \\cite{liu2019_roberta} end-to-end (lr$\\,{=}\\,2{\\times}10^{-5}$, 3 epochs, 5-fold CV) on Pythia-1.4B nucleus data.\nBoth converge to ${\\sim}$59\\% (RoBERTa 59.5$\\pm$3.2\\%, DeBERTa 58.3$\\pm$4.7\\%), 19pp below feature-based RF (78.2\\%); the 1.2pp inter-backbone gap rules out architecture selection bias.\nBoth easily separate d0 (F1$>$0.95) but fail at adjacent-depth discrimination (d1/d2 F1$<$0.42).\nProbing reveals a negative correlation between PLM attention and RF permutation importance for both backbones (DeBERTa $\\rho{=}{-}0.484$, RoBERTa $\\rho{=}{-}0.456$; zero top-5 overlap; Appendix~\\ref{app:feature_overlap}), confirming that PLMs encode surface lexical cues while our features capture perplexity tail structure inaccessible to end-to-end learning.\n\nDCOL \\cite{cloc2025}, which adds a contrastive ordinal loss, fared worse: across six iterations with two backbones (Appendix~\\ref{app:dcol_iterations}), the best run (RoBERTa-large) reached only 64.5\\% with severe overfitting, 13.7pp below RF.\nThat all end-to-end approaches---standard finetuning (58--60\\%), DCOL (64.5\\%)---substantially underperform feature-based methods (78.2\\%) confirms that depth estimation requires explicit distributional statistics rather than learned text representations.",

  "session_id": "c5410c02-cfc4-4643-ac2a-000192cebef1",

  "tool_use_result": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_2/projects/recursive_gen_depth_est/docs/paper/section_appendix.tex",
    "oldString": "sec:atlas:trajectories",
    "newString": "sec:atlas:strategy",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Additional Results}\n\\label{app:additional}\n\n\\subsection{Top-$k$ Feature Trajectories}\n\\label{app:topk_trajectories}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:feature_trajectory} in the main text shows feature trajectories for nucleus ($p{=}0.95$) and temperature ($\\tau{=}0.9$) sampling.\nTop-$k$ ($k{=}50$) trajectories follow the same monotonically decreasing trend for all features but with compressed inter-depth spacing, consistent with the signal attenuation effect discussed in \\S\\ref{sec:atlas:trajectories}.\nConcretely, the d0$\\to$d1 drop in \\textsc{p90\\_ppl} under top-$k$ is 78\\% (vs.\\ 87\\% for nucleus), and the d2$\\to$d3 reduction is 31\\% (vs.\\ 39\\% for nucleus).\nDespite the magnitude reduction, feature rank ordering across depths is preserved, which explains why ordinal classifiers maintain directionality even under top-$k$ conditions.\n\n\\subsection{Permutation Importance}\n\\label{app:permutation}\n\nTo verify that the Gini-based importance rankings in \\S\\ref{sec:atlas:features} are not an artifact of the impurity measure, we compute permutation importance (5-fold CV, 10 repeats) for all 9 atlas cells.\nResults are highly consistent: \\textsc{p90\\_ppl} ranks in the top 2 in 9/9 cells under both measures, and \\textsc{var\\_surprisal} in 8/9 (vs.\\ 7/9 for Gini, with the additional cell being OLMo top-$k$).\nThe mean Spearman rank correlation between Gini and permutation importance vectors across all 9 cells is $\\rho{=}0.94$, confirming that the reported feature universality is robust to the choice of importance measure.\n\n\\subsection{DCOL Iteration Details}\n\\label{app:dcol_iterations}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:dcol_iterations} summarizes all six DCOL iterations.\nDeBERTa-v3-large exhibited systematic numerical instability across five configurations; RoBERTa-large resolved stability but could not close the accuracy gap with feature-based methods.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{DCOL iteration summary. All experiments use Pythia-1.4B nucleus data. RF baseline: 78.2\\%.}\n\\label{tab:dcol_iterations}\n\\begin{tabular}{llll}\n\\toprule\nVer. & Backbone & Config & Outcome \\\\\n\\midrule\nv1 & DeBERTa-large & Full FT, margin\\_lr$\\,{=}\\,10^{-3}$ & Embed.\\ collapse (acc 31.5\\%) \\\\\nv2 & DeBERTa-large & Full FT, margin\\_lr$\\,{=}\\,10^{-4}$ & OOM (GPU contention) \\\\\nv3 & DeBERTa-large & Full FT, margin\\_lr$\\,{=}\\,10^{-4}$ & Forgetting $+$ OOM \\\\\nv4 & DeBERTa-large & LoRA $r{=}16$, m\\_lr$\\,{=}\\,10^{-3}$ & Embed.\\ collapse (v1 repeat) \\\\\nv5 & DeBERTa-large & LoRA $r{=}16$, m\\_lr$\\,{=}\\,10^{-4}$ & NaN gradient explosion \\\\\nv6 & RoBERTa-large & Full FT, grad clip 1.0 & Stable; acc 64.5\\% ($-$13.7pp) \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Feature Overlap Analysis}\n\\label{app:feature_overlap}\n\nWe probe whether end-to-end PLM representations capture the same depth signals as our statistical features.\nDeBERTa attention importance and RF permutation importance exhibit a significant negative correlation (Spearman $\\rho{=}{-}0.484$, $p{=}0.036$; top-5 Jaccard overlap$\\,{=}\\,0$).\nPLM representations excel at surface lexical features (TTR: $R^2{=}0.85$, repeated bigrams) but fail to capture perplexity distribution statistics---\\textsc{var\\_surprisal} and \\textsc{p90\\_ppl} yield negative $R^2$ under linear probing.\nThis confirms that the 19pp accuracy gap between feature-based and end-to-end methods (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:e2e}) stems from complementary---not redundant---representations: PLMs capture lexical patterns while the statistical features capture distributional tail properties that constitute the dominant depth markers.\n",

1	\section{The Depth Degradation Atlas}
2	\label{sec:atlas}
3	
4	We present the Depth Degradation Atlas: a systematic empirical map of how statistical fingerprints evolve across recursive generation depths, models, and decoding strategies.
5	The atlas spans $3 \times 3 = 9$ model--strategy cells evaluated at depths $d \in \{0, 1, 2, 3\}$, with depth extensions to $d{=}5$ for cross-model EDD validation (\S\ref{sec:atlas:edd}).
6	
7	%% ----------------------------------------------------------------
8	\subsection{In-Distribution Performance}
9	\label{sec:atlas:id}
10	
11	Table~\ref{tab:main_matrix} reports four-class Random Forest accuracy (5-fold CV, 200 trees) across all nine cells.
12	Accuracy ranges from 69.6\% (Pythia, top-$k$) to 85.4\% (GPT-2~XL, temperature), all exceeding the 25\% chance baseline by a wide margin.
13	Two axis effects emerge.
14	GPT-2~XL achieves the highest accuracy under every strategy (76.6--85.4\%), followed by OLMo (74.2--82.0\%) and Pythia (69.6--78.2\%).
15	Top-$k$ sampling yields the lowest accuracy in every model.
16	The mean within-model strategy gap is 8.4~pp, comparable to the 7.1~pp mean within-strategy model gap---decoding strategy and model family contribute roughly equally to performance variance.
17	
18	\begin{table}[t]
19	\centering
20	\small
21	\caption{Four-class RF accuracy (\%) across the $3 \times 3$ atlas. Rows: model families; columns: decoding strategies. Chance level is 25\%.}
22	\label{tab:main_matrix}
23	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
24	\toprule
25	& Nucleus & Temp.\ $\tau{=}0.9$ & Top-$k$ \\
26	\midrule
27	Pythia-1.4B  & 78.2 & 76.3 & 69.6 \\
28	OLMo-1B     & 78.8 & 82.0 & 74.2 \\
29	GPT-2~XL    & 83.3 & 85.4 & 76.6 \\
30	\bottomrule
31	\end{tabular}
32	\end{table}
33	
34	%% ----------------------------------------------------------------
35	\subsection{Feature Universality}
36	\label{sec:atlas:features}
37	
38	Despite the 15.8~pp accuracy spread across cells, a small feature family dominates in every setting.
39	The 90th-percentile perplexity (\texttt{p90\_ppl}) ranks among the top-2 most important features in all 9 cells (100\%); surprisal variance (\texttt{var\_surprisal}) reaches top-2 in 7 of 9 cells (78\%).
40	Table~\ref{tab:feature_importance} shows the full per-cell ranking.
41	
42	Cross-model feature-rank Spearman correlation averages $\bar{\rho} = 0.868$ (range $[0.619, 0.954]$): models agree on which features carry depth information even when they differ in absolute accuracy.
43	Among strategies, top-$k$ yields the highest cross-model rank correlation ($\rho = 0.811$)---the deterministic vocabulary truncation imposes a more uniform feature landscape across architectures, at the cost of weaker overall signal (\S\ref{sec:atlas:strategy}).
44	
45	Percentile-based features outperform their moment-based counterparts.
46	Token-level perplexity distributions are heavy-tailed: individual outlier passages can dominate the arithmetic mean of their depth class, whereas \texttt{p90\_ppl} is robust to such extremes while still capturing the tail erosion that signals recursive depth.
47	
48	The feature hierarchy also shifts with depth.
49	At shallow boundaries (d1 vs.\ d2), \texttt{var\_surprisal} dominates importance; at deeper boundaries (d3 vs.\ d4, d4 vs.\ d5), tail perplexity statistics (\texttt{p90\_ppl}, \texttt{kurtosis\_ppl}) take over.
50	This transition aligns with model-collapse dynamics~\cite{shumailov2024_nature}: early recursion narrows the surprisal distribution (captured by its variance), while later steps erode the perplexity tail (captured by upper quantiles and kurtosis).
51

56	\label{tab:feature_importance}

71	\label{fig:atlas_heatmap}

73	
74	%% ----------------------------------------------------------------
75	\subsection{Monotonicity and Inter-Depth Distances}
76	\label{sec:atlas:monotonicity}
77	
78	Depth features exhibit strong monotonic trends.
79	Spearman correlations between cell-level feature medians and depth $d$ range from $\rho = -0.82$ to $-0.89$ for perplexity-family features across all 9 cells.
80	Two complementary signal groups drive this structure.
81	Perplexity tail statistics (\texttt{p90\_ppl}, \texttt{mean\_surprisal}) decrease monotonically with depth ($\rho = -1.0$ across cell-level medians), reflecting the narrowing of the token-predictability distribution under recursive finetuning.
82	Repetition features (\texttt{rep\_2gram}, \texttt{rep\_3gram}, \texttt{rep\_4gram}) increase monotonically, capturing the growing self-repetition that accompanies distribution collapse.
83	
84	Inter-depth distances are non-uniform.
85	Binary d0-vs-d1 AUC exceeds 0.99 in all 9 cells, whereas d2-vs-d3 AUC ranges from 0.69 to 0.89.
86	The $d{=}0 \to d{=}1$ gap reflects the qualitative jump from human-written to machine-generated text; subsequent recursion steps produce progressively smaller distributional shifts.
87	This non-uniformity has practical implications: distinguishing real from first-generation synthetic text is near-trivial, but separating adjacent deep generations requires increasingly sensitive features---precisely the tail statistics identified in \S\ref{sec:atlas:features}.
88

Middle: \texttt{var\_surprisal} (decreasing). Bottom: \texttt{rep\_3gram} (increasing). Shaded bands: interquartile range. The $d{=}0 \to d{=}1$ gap exceeds all subsequent inter-depth gaps.}
93	\label{fig:feature_trajectory}
94	\end{figure}
95	
96	%% ----------------------------------------------------------------
97	\subsection{EDD Analysis}
98	\label{sec:atlas:edd}
99	
100	We extend the benchmark to depth $d{=}5$ for all three models under nucleus sampling.
101	Each model's depth-3 LoRA checkpoint is used to finetune and generate depth-4 data, and the process repeats for depth~5; feature extraction uses the same reference model (GPT-2 base, 124M).
102	We train dedicated binary RF classifiers (500 trees) for each adjacent pair, as defined in Eq.~\ref{eq:edd}.
103	
104	\paragraph{AUC decay profile.}
105	Figure~\ref{fig:auc_decay} plots the binary pairwise AUC decay curves.
106	For Pythia-1.4B (nucleus), AUC decays monotonically: d0-vs-d1 $= 0.999$, d1-vs-d2 $= 0.925$, d2-vs-d3 $= 0.823$, d3-vs-d4 $= 0.784$, d4-vs-d5 $= 0.683$.
107	The steepest drop occurs between the d1-vs-d2 and d2-vs-d3 pairs; the curve then flattens as the system approaches an indistinguishable regime gradually rather than crossing a sharp boundary.
108	
109	\paragraph{Cross-model convergence at depth 4--5.}
110	All three models exhibit a consistent profile at the deep boundaries.
111	The d3-vs-d4 AUC clusters near the $T{=}0.75$ threshold: Pythia $0.784$ (bootstrap 95\% CI $[0.765, 0.802]$, $p = 0.001$), OLMo $0.765$ ($[0.744, 0.785]$, $p = 0.091$), GPT-2~XL $0.750$ ($[0.728, 0.771]$, $p = 0.505$), where $p$ denotes the one-tailed bootstrap probability under $H_0\!: \mathrm{AUC} \leq 0.75$ (1{,}000 iterations).
112	Only Pythia achieves significance at conventional levels; OLMo is marginal and GPT-2~XL falls on the threshold despite its higher in-distribution accuracy.
113	The d4-vs-d5 AUC falls uniformly below 0.70 across all three models (Pythia $0.683$, OLMo $0.684$, GPT-2~XL $0.681$), confirming a model-independent signal saturation point at depth~5.
114	
115	This pattern---directional consistency across models with graded statistical strength---supports interpreting the d3--d4 region as a \emph{transition zone} rather than a binary boundary.
116	The continuous AUC decay curve provides a more informative characterization of depth discriminability than any single $\mathrm{EDD}_T$ value.
117	
118	\paragraph{EDD threshold sensitivity.}
119	Table~\ref{tab:edd_sensitivity} reports $\mathrm{EDD}_T$ across the primary 9-cell benchmark (depths 0--3) at four threshold values.
120	At $T{=}0.65$, all 9 cells achieve $\mathrm{EDD} = 3$; at $T{=}0.75$, 7 of 9 cells maintain $\mathrm{EDD} \geq 3$; at $T{=}0.80$, the count drops to 5 of 9.
121	The cells losing $\mathrm{EDD}{=}3$ first are consistently the top-$k$ configurations (Pythia top-$k$ at $T{=}0.70$, then OLMo top-$k$ at $T{=}0.75$), where the d2-vs-d3 pair is the universal bottleneck.
122

124	\centering
125	\small
126	\caption{Number of atlas cells achieving $\mathrm{EDD} \geq 3$ at each threshold $T$, across 9 model--strategy cells (depths 0--3). The d2-vs-d3 boundary is the bottleneck; top-$k$ cells drop first.}
127	\label{tab:edd_sensitivity}
128	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}
129	\toprule
130	& $T{=}0.65$ & $T{=}0.70$ & $T{=}0.75$ & $T{=}0.80$ \\
131	\midrule
132	Cells with $\mathrm{EDD} \geq 3$ & 9/9 & 8/9 & 7/9 & 5/9 \\

136

140	\caption{Binary pairwise AUC decay curves for three models (nucleus sampling, depths 0--5). Horizontal dashed lines mark $T \in \{0.65, 0.70, 0.75, 0.80\}$. All three curves converge below 0.70 at d4-vs-d5, while d3-vs-d4 AUC clusters in the range 0.750--0.784. Shaded bands: bootstrap 95\% CIs.}
141	\label{fig:auc_decay}
142	\end{figure}
143	
144	\paragraph{Decision boundary dilution.}
145	\label{sec:atlas:edd:dilution}
146	EDD's binary-pair design is motivated by \emph{decision boundary dilution}: when a multi-class classifier must simultaneously separate $K$ depths, its decision boundaries are shared across all $\binom{K}{2}$ class pairs, diluting the capacity allocated to any single adjacent pair.
147	Concretely, in the Pythia nucleus setting (experiment \texttt{granularity\_ablation\_rf\_001}), the d2\,vs.\,d3 pairwise AUC degrades from 0.804 in a 4-class pipeline to 0.689 in a 6-class pipeline---a 14.3\% relative decline---despite identical underlying data.
148	Mean classification accuracy likewise drops from 99.5\% (binary) to 78.3\% (4-class) to 62.9\% (6-class).
149	Because the pairwise signal between depths $d$ and $d{+}1$ does not change when unrelated depths are added, this degradation is entirely attributable to boundary dilution, not signal loss.
150	Binary pairwise classifiers eliminate this confound, isolating the true adjacent-pair discriminability that EDD is designed to measure.
151	
152	%% ----------------------------------------------------------------
153	\subsection{Strategy-Dependent Trajectories}
154	\label{sec:atlas:strategy}
155	
156	The three decoding strategies induce qualitatively distinct degradation trajectories that resist reduction to a binary taxonomy.
157	
158	\paragraph{Nucleus ($p{=}0.95$): stable baseline.}
159	Nucleus sampling produces the most consistent accuracy across models (78.2--83.3\%), with moderate AUC decay and no anomalous pairwise boundaries.
160	It serves as the reference trajectory against which temperature and top-$k$ effects are measured.
161	
162	\paragraph{Temperature ($\tau{=}0.9$): signal enhancement with boundary reversal.}
163	Temperature sampling yields the highest accuracy in 2 of 3 models (GPT-2~XL $85.4\%$, OLMo $82.0\%$) and the second-highest in Pythia ($76.3\%$).
164	Unlike nucleus and top-$k$, which truncate the vocabulary, temperature uniformly scales logits, redistributing probability mass across all tokens.
165	This accelerates convergence to an attractor distribution during recursive finetuning~\cite{shumailov2024_nature}, amplifying the statistical fingerprint at each depth step.
166	The acceleration manifests as a systematic depth shift: temperature depth-$N$ features approximate nucleus/top-$k$ depth-$(N{+}1)$ features.
167	For GPT-2~XL, temperature depth-1 \texttt{p90\_ppl} median is 92.2, falling within the nucleus/top-$k$ depth-2 range of 65--92.
168	
169	This depth shift creates a boundary reversal risk in cross-strategy transfer (\S\ref{sec:generalization}): a classifier trained on nucleus and top-$k$ data systematically overpredicts depth for temperature-generated text, because temperature depth-1 features overlap with nucleus/top-$k$ depth-2 features.
170	
171	\paragraph{Top-$k$ ($k{=}50$): signal attenuation, direction preserved.}
172	Top-$k$ sampling produces the lowest accuracy in every model (69.6--76.6\%), with a mean 8.4~pp gap from the strongest strategy.
173	Hard vocabulary truncation removes low-probability tokens that carry depth-discriminative information, attenuating the overall signal.
174	The \emph{direction} of feature evolution, however, is preserved: perplexity statistics still decrease monotonically with depth, and feature importance rankings maintain the highest cross-model correlation ($\rho = 0.811$).
175	Top-$k$ compresses the depth signal without distorting it.
176	
177	\paragraph{Strategy vs.\ model effect.}
178	The strategy effect (8.4~pp range) exceeds the model effect (7.1~pp range) across the atlas, indicating that deployment-time knowledge of the decoding strategy is at least as important as knowledge of the generator model for accurate depth estimation.
179

The perplexity-tail and surprisal-variance blocks maintain high importance across all cells.}\n\\label{fig:atlas_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Monotonicity and Inter-Depth Distances}\n\\label{sec:atlas:monotonicity}\n\nDepth features exhibit strong monotonic trends.\nSpearman correlations between cell-level feature medians and depth $d$ range from $\\rho = -0.82$ to $-0.89$ for perplexity-family features across all 9 cells.\nTwo complementary signal groups drive this structure.\nPerplexity tail statistics (\\texttt{p90\\_ppl}, \\texttt{mean\\_surprisal}) decrease monotonically with depth ($\\rho = -1.0$ across cell-level medians), reflecting the narrowing of the token-predictability distribution under recursive finetuning.\nRepetition features (\\texttt{rep\\_2gram}, \\texttt{rep\\_3gram}, \\texttt{rep\\_4gram}) increase monotonically, capturing the growing self-repetition that accompanies distribution collapse.\n\nInter-depth distances are non-uniform.\nBinary d0-vs-d1 AUC exceeds 0.99 in all 9 cells, whereas d2-vs-d3 AUC ranges from 0.69 to 0.89.\nThe $d{=}0 \\to d{=}1$ gap reflects the qualitative jump from human-written to machine-generated text; subsequent recursion steps produce progressively smaller distributional shifts.\nThis non-uniformity has practical implications: distinguishing real from first-generation synthetic text is near-trivial, but separating adjacent deep generations requires increasingly sensitive features---precisely the tail statistics identified in \\S\\ref{sec:atlas:features}.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n% \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/feature_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{Feature trajectories across depths $d{=}0$ to $d{=}3$, stratified by model (columns) and strategy (line style). Top row: \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} (monotonically decreasing). Middle: \\texttt{var\\_surprisal} (decreasing). Bottom: \\texttt{rep\\_3gram} (increasing). Shaded bands: interquartile range. The $d{=}0 \\to d{=}1$ gap exceeds all subsequent inter-depth gaps.}\n\\label{fig:feature_trajectory}\n\\end{figure}\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{EDD Analysis}\n\\label{sec:atlas:edd}\n\nWe extend the benchmark to depth $d{=}5$ for all three models under nucleus sampling.\nEach model's depth-3 LoRA checkpoint is used to finetune and generate depth-4 data, and the process repeats for depth~5; feature extraction uses the same reference model (GPT-2 base, 124M).\nWe train dedicated binary RF classifiers (500 trees) for each adjacent pair, as defined in Eq.~\\ref{eq:edd}.\n\n\\paragraph{AUC decay profile.}\nFigure~\\ref{fig:auc_decay} plots the binary pairwise AUC decay curves.\nFor Pythia-1.4B (nucleus), AUC decays monotonically: d0-vs-d1 $= 0.999$, d1-vs-d2 $= 0.925$, d2-vs-d3 $= 0.823$, d3-vs-d4 $= 0.784$, d4-vs-d5 $= 0.683$.\nThe steepest drop occurs between the d1-vs-d2 and d2-vs-d3 pairs; the curve then flattens as the system approaches an indistinguishable regime gradually rather than crossing a sharp boundary.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model convergence at depth 4--5.}\nAll three models exhibit a consistent profile at the deep boundaries.\nThe d3-vs-d4 AUC clusters near the $T{=}0.75$ threshold: Pythia $0.784$ (bootstrap 95\\% CI $[0.765, 0.802]$, $p = 0.001$), OLMo $0.765$ ($[0.744, 0.785]$, $p = 0.091$), GPT-2~XL $0.750$ ($[0.728, 0.771]$, $p = 0.505$), where $p$ denotes the one-tailed bootstrap probability under $H_0\\!: \\mathrm{AUC} \\leq 0.75$ (1{,}000 iterations).\nOnly Pythia achieves significance at conventional levels; OLMo is marginal and GPT-2~XL falls on the threshold despite its higher in-distribution accuracy.\nThe d4-vs-d5 AUC falls uniformly below 0.70 across all three models (Pythia $0.683$, OLMo $0.684$, GPT-2~XL $0.681$), confirming a model-independent signal saturation point at depth~5.\n\nThis pattern---directional consistency across models with graded statistical strength---supports interpreting the d3--d4 region as a \\emph{transition zone} rather than a binary boundary.\nThe continuous AUC decay curve provides a more informative characterization of depth discriminability than any single $\\mathrm{EDD}_T$ value.\n\n\\paragraph{EDD threshold sensitivity.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:edd_sensitivity} reports $\\mathrm{EDD}_T$ across the primary 9-cell benchmark (depths 0--3) at four threshold values.\nAt $T{=}0.65$, all 9 cells achieve $\\mathrm{EDD} = 3$; at $T{=}0.75$, 7 of 9 cells maintain $\\mathrm{EDD} \\geq 3$; at $T{=}0.80$, the count drops to 5 of 9.\nThe cells losing $\\mathrm{EDD}{=}3$ first are consistently the top-$k$ configurations (Pythia top-$k$ at $T{=}0.70$, then OLMo top-$k$ at $T{=}0.75$), where the d2-vs-d3 pair is the universal bottleneck.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Number of atlas cells achieving $\\mathrm{EDD} \\geq 3$ at each threshold $T$, across 9 model--strategy cells (depths 0--3). The d2-vs-d3 boundary is the bottleneck; top-$k$ cells drop first.}\n\\label{tab:edd_sensitivity}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n& $T{=}0.65$ & $T{=}0.70$ & $T{=}0.75$ & $T{=}0.80$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nCells with $\\mathrm{EDD} \\geq 3$ & 9/9 & 8/9 & 7/9 & 5/9 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n% \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/auc_decay.pdf}\n\\caption{Binary pairwise AUC decay curves for three models (nucleus sampling, depths 0--5). Horizontal dashed lines mark $T \\in \\{0.65, 0.70, 0.75, 0.80\\}$. All three curves converge below 0.70 at d4-vs-d5, while d3-vs-d4 AUC clusters in the range 0.750--0.784. Shaded bands: bootstrap 95\\% CIs.}\n\\label{fig:auc_decay}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Decision boundary dilution.}\n\\label{sec:atlas:edd:dilution}\nEDD's binary-pair design is motivated by \\emph{decision boundary dilution}: when a multi-class classifier must simultaneously separate $K$ depths, its decision boundaries are shared across all $\\binom{K}{2}$ class pairs, diluting the capacity allocated to any single adjacent pair.\nConcretely, in the Pythia nucleus setting (experiment \\texttt{granularity\\_ablation\\_rf\\_001}), the d2\\,vs.\\,d3 pairwise AUC degrades from 0.804 in a 4-class pipeline to 0.689 in a 6-class pipeline---a 14.3\\% relative decline---despite identical underlying data.\nMean classification accuracy likewise drops from 99.5\\% (binary) to 78.3\\% (4-class) to 62.9\\% (6-class).\nBecause the pairwise signal between depths $d$ and $d{+}1$ does not change when unrelated depths are added, this degradation is entirely attributable to boundary dilution, not signal loss.\nBinary pairwise classifiers eliminate this confound, isolating the true adjacent-pair discriminability that EDD is designed to measure.\n\n%% ----------------------------------------------------------------\n\\subsection{Strategy-Dependent Trajectories}\n\\label{sec:atlas:strategy}\n\nThe three decoding strategies induce qualitatively distinct degradation trajectories that resist reduction to a binary taxonomy.\n\n\\paragraph{Nucleus ($p{=}0.95$): stable baseline.}\nNucleus sampling produces the most consistent accuracy across models (78.2--83.3\\%), with moderate AUC decay and no anomalous pairwise boundaries.\nIt serves as the reference trajectory against which temperature and top-$k$ effects are measured.\n\n\\paragraph{Temperature ($\\tau{=}0.9$): signal enhancement with boundary reversal.}\nTemperature sampling yields the highest accuracy in 2 of 3 models (GPT-2~XL $85.4\\%$, OLMo $82.0\\%$) and the second-highest in Pythia ($76.3\\%$).\nUnlike nucleus and top-$k$, which truncate the vocabulary, temperature uniformly scales logits, redistributing probability mass across all tokens.\nThis accelerates convergence to an attractor distribution during recursive finetuning~\\cite{shumailov2024_nature}, amplifying the statistical fingerprint at each depth step.\nThe acceleration manifests as a systematic depth shift: temperature depth-$N$ features approximate nucleus/top-$k$ depth-$(N{+}1)$ features.\nFor GPT-2~XL, temperature depth-1 \\texttt{p90\\_ppl} median is 92.2, falling within the nucleus/top-$k$ depth-2 range of 65--92.\n\nThis depth shift creates a boundary reversal risk in cross-strategy transfer (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}): a classifier trained on nucleus and top-$k$ data systematically overpredicts depth for temperature-generated text, because temperature depth-1 features overlap with nucleus/top-$k$ depth-2 features.\n\n\\paragraph{Top-$k$ ($k{=}50$): signal attenuation, direction preserved.}\nTop-$k$ sampling produces the lowest accuracy in every model (69.6--76.6\\%), with a mean 8.4~pp gap from the strongest strategy.\nHard vocabulary truncation removes low-probability tokens that carry depth-discriminative information, attenuating the overall signal.\nThe \\emph{direction} of feature evolution, however, is preserved: perplexity statistics still decrease monotonically with depth, and feature importance rankings maintain the highest cross-model correlation ($\\rho = 0.811$).\nTop-$k$ compresses the depth signal without distorting it.\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy vs.\\ model effect.}\nThe strategy effect (8.4~pp range) exceeds the model effect (7.1~pp range) across the atlas, indicating that deployment-time knowledge of the decoding strategy is at least as important as knowledge of the generator model for accurate depth estimation.\n",

    }
  }
}
